Fortunately I sank at once to rest, and with the morning was born the clear discernment that whatever I ought to do, it was more manly of me to go than to write to Ellen. Accordingly, I made arrangements for getting somebody to supply my place in the pulpit for a couple of Sundays, and went home.

===Chinese===

“我的好先生，你正在同那灵魂的大敌讨价还价。哦！你若知道——你*能*知道就好了——你躲避他时定会斩钉截铁，如同脚下突然裂开地狱时那样毫不迟疑。别管将来。你唯一要做的，是那件离你最近、神为你安排的事。《诗篇》119 篇怎么说？——‘你的话是我脚前的灯。’应许给我们的光，并不能照亮百里之外的路，却照亮下一步的脚；走了这一步，再一步的光也随之而来。全能者的默示临到我，也不能把我传给你的信息说得更明白了。恕我直言——我知道你是牧师，或许我不该这样同你说话，可我是个老太婆了。倘若不是想到这故事能救你脱离那比死更糟的东西，你永远不会从我口中听到我的历史。”

这时有人敲门，阿伯小姐的妹妹走了进来。寒暄几句之后，我便告辞步行回家。我整个人都懵了。谁能想见，那张安详的脸背后竟藏着这样悲剧的深渊，她那井然的刻板，竟像火山土上的青草鲜花，底下蛰伏着维苏威的烈火？我算是全然看错了人；而我由此受了一课——此后又一再再受的一课——在男男女女的身上，覆盖着平常外表的，是阳光从未照入的无名深渊，唯有极难得的机缘才使它显露。

可我的念头几乎立刻又转回自己身上，怎么也下不了决心。我虚弱而疲惫，越想越拿不定任何主意。一夜辗转之后，早晨我的处境更窘，既然什么事也做不成，便逃到我惯常的避难所——海边去了。一整天我摇来摆去，全然没有能力在把我向相反方向拉扯的两种矛盾冲动之间作一裁断。

我知道该怎么做，可埃伦的身影总在我眼前，无言地控诉着将加于她的不义；我想象着她被抛弃、一辈子被毁掉的样子。我对自己说，本能固然不错，可若不拿理性来推究，理性又何必赐给我们；而推究大体上正是对所谓本能、对仓促的第一印象的校正。我知道许多例子，男女彼此相爱而意见并不相同；说到底，在神学批评上意见一致，本是一种可怜的结合纽带。可是这一层才申诉完，问题的另一面又带着它全部的分明摆了出来，正如阿伯小姐所呈现的那样。

我回到家里，疲惫得全军覆没似的，倒头便睡。所幸我立刻沉入了休息；随着清晨到来，一个明白的判断也生了：且不说该做什么，亲自去见埃伦总比写信给她更有男子气。于是，我安排好找人代我登坛讲两个星期日的道，随后回了家。

## IX Oxford Street / 第九章 牛津街

===Original===
Until I had actually left, I hardly knew where I was going, but at last I made up my mind I would go to Reuben Shapcott, another fellow-student, whom I knew to be living in lodgings in one of the streets just then beginning to creep over the unoccupied ground between Camden Town and Haverstock Hill, near the Chalk Farm turnpike gate. To his address I betook myself, and found him not at home. He, like me, had been unsuccessful as a minister, and wrote a London letter for two country papers, making up about £100 or £120 a year by preaching occasionally in small Unitarian chapels in the country. I waited till his return, and told him my story. He advised me to take a bed in the house where he was staying, and to consider what could be done.
At first I thought I would consult Mardon, but I could not bring myself to go near him. How was I to behave in Mary’s presence? During the last few months she had been so continually before me, that it would have been absolutely impossible for me to treat her with assumed indifference. I could not have trusted myself to attempt it. When I had been lying alone and awake at night, I had thought of all the endless miles of hill and valley that lay outside my window, separating me from the one house in which I could be at peace; and at times I scarcely prevented myself from getting up and taking the mail train and presenting myself at Mardon’s door, braving all consequences. With the morning light, however, would come cooler thoughts and a dull sense of impossibility.
This, I know, was not pure love for her; it was a selfish passion for relief. But then I have never known what is meant by a perfectly pure love. When Christian was in the Valley of the Shadow of Death, and, being brought to the mouth of hell, was forced to put up his sword, and could do no other than cry, O Lord, I beseech Thee, deliver my soul, he heard a voice going before him and saying, Though I walk through the Valley of the Shadow of Death, I will fear none ill, for Thou art with me. And by and by the day broke. “Then,” said Christian, “He hath turned the Shadow of Death into morning. Whereupon Christian sang﻿—

> “Oh, world of wonders! (I can say no less)
 

 That I should be preserved in that distress
 

 That I have met with here! Oh, blessed be
 

 That hand that from it hath delivered me!”


===Chinese===
直到真正动身，我还不大知道自己要去哪里，但终于拿定主意，去投鲁本·沙普科特（Reuben Shapcott）——他是我昔日的另一位同学，当时寄住在卡姆登镇（Camden Town）与哈弗斯托克山（Haverstock Hill）之间的空地上新辟的一条街上；那一带的街道，彼时正开始向乔克农场（Chalk Farm）收费站关卡附近的闲置地皮蔓延。我寻到他的住处，他却不在家。他同我一样，做牧师不曾得意，为两家外省报纸撰写伦敦通信，又在乡间各小型一位论礼拜堂偶或讲道，凑成每年约 100 至 120 镑的收入。我等到他回来，把自己的经历讲给他听。他劝我在他寄宿的那家人家赁一张床位，再慢慢商量如何是好。
起初我想去找马登商量，却怎么也迈不开步子走近他。在玛丽面前，我该拿出怎样的举止呢？过去几个月里，她无时无刻不浮现在我眼前，要我装出无动于衷来对待她，是绝对办不到的。我不敢信自己真去尝试。夜里独自清醒地躺着时，我想过窗外那无穷无尽的山峦与谷地，一英里又一英里，把我同唯一能给我安宁的那座房子隔开；有几回，我几乎按捺不住要起身去搭邮车，径直去敲马登家的门，把一切后果置之度外。然而晨光一到，心思总会冷静下来，只剩一种沉沉的、不可能之感。
我知道，这并非对她纯粹的爱，而是一种渴求解脱的自私的激情。可是话又说回来，我从来不曾领会过所谓全然纯粹的爱究竟何所指。当日基督徒（Christian）行在死荫幽谷之中，被带到地狱门口，被迫收剑入鞘，别无他法，唯有呼求：主啊，求你救我的灵魂——这时他听见有声音在他前头行，说：我虽然行过死荫的幽谷，也不怕遭害，因为你与我同在。不多时，天就破了晓。“于是，”基督徒说，“他已使死荫化为清晨。”基督徒便唱道——

> “哦，奇迹的世界！（我只能如此说）
 

 我竟能在那般的危难中得保全
 

 那正是我在此处所遭遇的！哦，愿颂赞
 

 归于那只把我从中拯救出来的手！”


===Original===
This was Christian’s love for God, and for God as his helper. Was that perfectly pure? However, this is a digression. I determined to help myself in my own way, and thought I would try the publishers. One morning I walked from Camden Town to Paternoster Row. I went straightway into two or three shops and asked whether they wanted anybody. I was ready to do the ordinary work of a publisher’s assistant, and aspired no higher. I met with several refusals, some of them not over-polite, and the degradation﻿—for so I felt it﻿—of wandering through the streets and suing for employment cut me keenly. I remember one man in particular, who spoke to me with the mechanical brutality with which probably he replied to a score of similar applications every week. He sat in a little glass box at the end of a long dark room lighted with gas. It was a bitterly cold room, with no contrivances for warming it, but in his box there was a fire burning for his own special benefit. He surveyed all his clerks unceasingly, and woe betide the unhappy wretch who was caught idling. He and his slaves reminded me of a thrashing-machine which is worked by horses walking round in a ring, the driver being perched on a high stool in the middle and armed with a long whip.
While I was waiting his pleasure he came out and spoke to one or two of his miserable subordinates words of directest and sharpest rebuke, without anger or the least loss of self-possession, and yet without the least attempt to mitigate their severity. I meditated much upon him. If ever I had occasion to rebuke anybody, I always did it apologetically, unless I happened to be in a flaming passion﻿—and this was my habit, not from any respectable motive of consideration for the person rebuked, but partly because I am timid, and partly because I shrink from giving pain. This man said with perfect ease what I could not have said unless I had been wrought up to white heat. With all my dislike to him, I envied him: I envied his complete certainty; for although his language was harsh in the extreme, he was always sure of his ground, and the victim upon whom his lash descended could never say that he had given absolutely no reason for the chastisement, and that it was altogether a mistake. I envied also his ability to make himself disagreeable and care nothing about it; his power to walk in his own path, and his resolve to succeed, no matter what the cost might be.
As I left him, it occurred to me that I might be more successful perhaps with a publisher of whom I had heard, who published and sold books of a sceptical turn. To him I accordingly went, and although I had no introductions or recommendatory letters, I was received, if not with a cordiality, at least with an interest which surprised me. He took me into a little back shop, and after hearing patiently what I wanted, he asked me somewhat abruptly what I thought of the miracles in the Bible. This was a curious question if he wished to understand my character; but his mind so constantly revolved in one circle, and existed so completely by hostility to the prevailing orthodoxy, that belief or disbelief in it was the standard by which he judged men. It was a very absurd standard doubtless, but no more absurd than many others, and not so absurd then as it would be now, when heresy is becoming more fashionable.

===Chinese===
这便是基督徒对上帝的爱，对作为救助者的上帝的爱。这难道是全然纯粹的吗？不过这是题外话了。我决意按自己的办法自救，想去出版商那里碰碰运气。一天早晨，我从卡姆登镇步行到帕特诺斯特街（Paternoster Row）。径直走进两三家铺子，问他们要不要人。出版商助手份内的寻常活计我都愿意干，也不敢有更高的想望。我接连遭到回绝，有几处还不算客气；那种沿街奔走、乞求职位的屈辱——我确实深深感到它是屈辱——狠狠剜着我的心。我特别记得一个人，他同我说话时那种机械的粗暴，想必正是他每周回覆二十来桩同类求职的口气。他坐在一间狭长的、煤气灯照明的暗屋尽头的一只小玻璃匣里。那屋子冷得刺骨，没有任何取暖的设备，唯有他那只匣子里，专为他自己烧着一炉火。他一刻不停地监视着全体职员，哪个倒霉鬼被撞见偷闲，便有苦头吃。他同他的奴隶们令我想起一架打谷机：马匹绕着圈行走带动机器，赶马人高踞在正中的高凳上，手中握一条长鞭。
我候他发落时，他走了出来，对一两个可怜的下属说出最直截、最尖刻的训斥，不动声色，绝无半分失态，却也绝无半分减轻其锋芒之意。我对这个人沉思良久。我若不得不训斥什么人，说起来总是带着歉意——除非恰好在盛怒之中——而这习惯并非出于体谅受斥者的什么可敬动机，一半因为我胆怯，一半因为我不忍给人痛苦。这人满不在乎地说出的那些话，除非我被激到白热的地步，是万万说不出口的。尽管我厌恶他，我却羡慕他：羡慕他那份全然的笃定；因为他的言语虽然苛酷之至，他对自己的立场却永远有把握，鞭子落到谁头上，那受刑的人也说不出自己全然没有招罚的理由，说不出这整件事纯属误会。我也羡慕他那种把人得罪了却浑不当回事的本事；羡慕他独行其道的力量，和不惜一切代价也要成功的心志。
离开他之后，我忽然想起或许可以去投一位久有耳闻的出版商，此人出版并发售带怀疑论倾向的书籍。我便去找了他；尽管无人引荐，也没有荐书，我受到的接待即便算不上热忱，至少那份关注也使我暗暗称奇。他把我领进后进的一间小铺面，耐心听完我的来意之后，颇为突兀地问我如何看待《圣经》里的那些神迹。他若想了解我的品性，这实在是个古怪的问题；但他的心智永远只在一个圆圈里打转，全然倚仗对盛行正统的敌意而活，信或不信那正统，便成了他衡量人的标尺。这标尺无疑荒唐，却也未见得比别的许多标尺更荒唐；而且在当年，还不像如今这样荒唐——如今异端已渐成时髦了。

===Original===
I explained to him as well as I could what my position was; that I did not suppose that the miracles actually happened as they are recorded, but that, generally speaking, the miracle was a very intense statement of a divine truth; in fact, a truth which was felt with a more than common intensity seemed to take naturally a miraculous expression. Hence, so far from neglecting the miraculous stories of the Bible as simply outside me, I rejoiced in them more, perhaps, than in the plain historical or didactic prose.
He seemed content, although hardly to comprehend, and the result was that he asked me if I would help him in his business. In order to do this, it would be more economical if I would live in his house, which was too big for him. He promised to give me £40 a year, in addition to board and lodging. I joyously assented, and the bargain was struck.
The next day I came to my new quarters. I found that he was a bachelor, with a niece, apparently about four or five and twenty years old, acting as a housekeeper, who assisted him in literary work. My own room was at the top of the house, warm, quiet, and comfortable, although the view was nothing but a wide reaching assemblage of chimney-pots. My hours were long﻿—from nine in the morning till seven in the evening; but this I did not mind. I felt that if I was not happy, I was at least protected, and that I was with a man who cared for me, and for whom I cared. The first day I went there, he said that I could have a fire in my bedroom whenever I chose, so that I could always retreat to it when I wished to be by myself. As for my duties, I was to sell his books, keep his accounts, read proofs, run errands, and in short do just what he did himself.
After my first morning’s work we went upstairs to dinner, and I was introduced to “my niece Theresa.” I was rather surprised that I should have been admitted to a house in which there lived a young woman with no mother nor aunt, but this surprise ceased when I came to know more of Theresa and her uncle. She had yellowish hair which was naturally waved, a big arched head, greyish-blue eyes, so far as I could make out, and a mouth which, although it had curves in it, was compressed and indicative of great force of character. She was rather short, with square shoulders, and she had a singularly vigorous, firm walk. She had a way, when she was not eating or drinking, of sitting back in her chair at table and looking straight at the person with whom she was talking.
Her uncle, whom, by the way, I had forgotten to name﻿—his name was Wollaston﻿—happened to know some popular preacher whom I knew, and I said that I wondered so many people went to hear him, for I believed him to be a hypocrite, and hypocrisy was one of the easiest of crimes to discover. Theresa, who had hitherto been silent, and was reclining in her usual attitude, instantly broke out with an emphasis and directness which quite startled me.

===Chinese===
我尽可能向他说明了自己的立场：我并不以为那些神迹真如记载所言地发生过；但大体说来，神迹乃是一条神圣真理的极其强烈的表述；事实上，一条被人以超乎寻常的强度感受到的真理，似乎自然而然便会采取神迹的表达形式。因此，我非但没有把《圣经》里那些神迹故事当作与己无关的东西撇在一旁，反而对它们的喜爱，说不定还超过那些平实的历史性或说教性的文字。
他似乎表示满意，尽管未必听懂了；结果是，他问我可愿帮他经营生意。为此，若我住到他家中——那房子对他一人来说太大——倒更为经济。他允诺除食宿之外，每年给我 40 镑。我欣然应允，买卖就此敲定。
次日我便迁入新居。我得知他是个单身汉，有一位年约二十四五的侄女充当管家，兼协助他做文字工作。我自己的房间在房子顶层，温暖、清静、舒适，只是窗外望出去，无非是绵延成片的一片烟囱林。工时很长——从上午九点做到晚上七点；这我倒不在乎。我觉得，纵然算不得快乐，至少有了庇护，身边是一个关怀我、也是我所关怀的人。头一天到那里，他就说，我的卧室里随时可以生火，只要我愿意；这样每当我想独处，总可以退避到那间屋里。至于职责，便是售书、记账、读校样、跑腿，一言以蔽之，他本人干的一切我都得干。
头一个上午的活计做完，我们上楼用餐，我被引见给“我的侄女特蕾莎”。这家人家里住着一位没有母亲也没有姨姑的年轻女子，而我竟被接纳进来，我不免有些诧异；但等我多了解了特蕾莎和她伯父一些，诧异便消失了。她的头发微黄，天然卷曲，头颅宽大而圆隆，眼睛依我辨认是灰蓝色，那张嘴虽有柔和的曲线，却抿得很紧，显示出性格中强大的力量。她个子偏矮，肩膀方正，走路的样子异样地矫健坚定。在饭桌上，只要不在吃吃喝喝，她惯于向椅背上一靠，直直望着与她交谈的人。
顺带说一句，我一直忘了提她伯父的名字——他姓沃拉斯顿（Wollaston）——他恰好认识某位我也认识的名讲道师；我说，真奇怪竟有那么多人去听他讲道，因为我认为他是个伪君子，而伪善乃是最容易识破的罪过之一。特蕾莎此前一直缄默，正以她惯常的姿势半倚半靠着，闻言立刻迸发出来，那斩钉截铁的直率把我吓了一跳。

===Original===
“The easiest to discover, do you think, Mr. Rutherford? I think it is the most difficult, at least for ordinary persons; and when they do discover it, I believe they like it, especially if it is successful. They like the sanction it gives to their own hypocrisy. They like a man to come to them who will say to them, ‘We are all hypocrites together,’ and who will put his finger to his nose and comfort them. Don’t you think so yourself?”
In conversation I was always a bad hand at assuming a position contrary to the one assumed by the person to whom I might be talking﻿—nor could I persistently maintain my own position if it happened to be opposed. I always rather tried to see as my opponent saw, and to discover how much there was in him with which I could sympathise. I therefore assented weakly to Theresa, and she seemed disappointed. Dinner was just over; she got up and rang the bell and went out of the room.
I found my work very hard, and some of it even loathsome. Particularly loathsome was that part of it which brought me into contact with the trade. I had to sell books to the booksellers’ assistants, and I had to collect books myself. These duties are usually undertaken in large establishments by men specially trained, who receive a low rate of wages and who are rather a rough set. It was totally different work to anything I had ever had to do before, and I suffered as a man with soft hands would suffer who was suddenly called to be a blacksmith or a dock-labourer.
Specially, too, did I miss the country. London lay round me like a mausoleum. I got into the habit of rising very early in the morning and walking out to Kensington Gardens and back before breakfast, varying my route occasionally so as even to reach Battersea Bridge, which was always a favourite spot with me. Kensington Gardens and Battersea Bridge were poor substitutes for the downs, and for the level stretch by the river towards the sea where I first saw Mardon, but we make too much of circumstances, and the very pressure of London produced a sensibility to whatever loveliness could be apprehended there, which was absent when loveliness was always around me. The stars seen in Oxford Street late one night; a sunset one summer evening from Lambeth pier; and, above everything, Piccadilly very early one summer morning, abide with me still, when much that was more romantic has been forgotten. On the whole, I was not unhappy. The constant outward occupation prevented any eating of the heart or undue brooding over problems which were insoluble, at least for my intellect, and on that very account fascinated me the more.

===Chinese===
“最容易识破，您真这样想吗，拉瑟福德先生？我以为它最难识破，至少对普通人而言是如此；而且即便识破了，我相信他们还喜欢它，尤其当它行之有效的时候。他们喜欢它给自己的伪善颁发的特许。他们喜欢有个人来到他们面前，对他们说：‘咱们全是伪君子’，一边把手指按在鼻子上，给他们安慰。您自己难道不这么想？”
交谈之中，要我站到与对话者相反的立场上，我一向是笨拙透顶——而倘若我自己的立场恰好遭到反对，我也坚持不住。我总是更倾向于像对手那样去看，去发现他身上有多少我能同情的东西。于是我有气无力地附和了特蕾莎，她似乎颇为失望。晚餐刚好结束；她站起身，摇了铃，走出了房间。
我发觉这活计非常辛苦，有些环节甚至可憎。最可憎的是那些使我同书业行当打交道的部分。我得向书店店员推销书籍，还得亲自上门收书。在大商号里，这些职务照例由受过专门训练的人担任，他们工钱微薄，是颇为粗悍的一类人。这工作同我此前不得不做的一切都截然不同；我吃的苦头，就好比一双娇嫩的手突然被拉去当铁匠或码头小工。
我还格外想念乡下。伦敦像一座陵墓四面围困着我。我养成了清早极早起身、早饭前步行到肯辛顿花园（Kensington Gardens）再走回来的习惯，偶尔变换路线，甚至一直走到巴特西桥（Battersea Bridge）——那素来是我心爱的一处所在。肯辛顿花园与巴特西桥，比起丘原、比起我初见马登的那段向着大海的河边平野，是蹩脚的替代品；但我们总把境遇看得太重，而正是伦敦的重压，反倒造出一种对那里凡可领会的美的敏感——当年美终日环绕我时，这种敏感倒是没有的。深夜在牛津街望见的星斗；一个夏日傍晚从兰贝斯码头（Lambeth pier）望见的落日；还有，比一切都更要紧的，一个夏日清晨极早时分的皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）大街——至今仍留驻我心，而许多当年更富浪漫情味的景物早已淡忘。总的说来，我并不算不快乐。持续向外的事务，拦住了内心的噬啮，也拦住了对那些无解问题——至少对我的智力而言无解——的过度沉潜；而正因其无解，它们愈发令我着魔。

===Original===
I do not think that Wollaston cared much for me personally. He was a curious compound, materialistic yet impulsive, and forever drawn to some new thing; without any love for anybody particularly, as far as I could see, and yet with much more general kindness and philanthropy than many a man possessing much stronger sympathies and antipathies. There was no holy of holies in him, into which one or two of the elect could occasionally be admitted and feel God to be there. He was no temple, but rather a comfortable, hospitable house open to all friends, well furnished with books and pictures, and free to every guest from garret to cellar. He had “liberal” notions about the relationship between the sexes. Not that he was a libertine, but he disbelieved in marriage, excepting for so long as husband and wife are a necessity to one another. If one should find the other uninteresting, or somebody else more interesting, he thought there ought to be a separation.
All this I soon learned from him, for he was communicative without any reserve. His treatment of his niece was peculiar. He would talk on all kinds of subjects before her, for he had a theory that she ought to receive precisely the same social training as men, and should know just what men knew. He was never coarse, but on the other hand he would say things to her in my presence which brought a flame into my face. What the evil consequences of this might be, I could not at once foresee, but one good result obviously was, that in his house there was nothing of that execrable practice of talking down to women; there was no change of level when women were present.
One day he began to speak about a novel which everybody was reading then, and I happened to say that I wished people who wrote novels would not write as if love were the very centre and sum of human existence. A man’s life was made up of so much besides love, and yet novelists were never weary of repeating the same story, telling it over and over again in a hundred different forms.
“I do not agree with you,” said Theresa. “I disagree with you utterly. I dislike foolish, inane sentiment﻿—it makes me sick; but I do believe, in the first place, that no man was ever good for anything who has not been devoured, I was going to say, by a great devotion to a woman. The lives of your great men are as much the history of women whom they adored as of themselves. Dante, Byron, Shelley, it is the same with all of them, and there is no mistake about it; it is the great fact of life. What would Shakespeare be without it? and Shakespeare is life. A man, worthy to be named a man, will find the fact of love perpetually confronting him till he reaches old age, and if he be not ruined by worldliness or dissipation, will be troubled by it when he is fifty as much as when he was twenty-five. It is the subject of all subjects. People abuse love, and think it the cause of half the mischief in the world. It is the one thing that keeps the world straight, and if it were not for that overpowering instinct, human nature would fall asunder; would be the prey of inconceivable selfishness and vices, and finally, there would be universal suicide. I did not intend to be eloquent: I hate being eloquent. But you did not mean what you said; you spoke from the head or teeth merely.”

===Chinese===
我不认为沃拉斯顿对我个人有多深的关切。他是个古怪的混合体：唯物，却又容易冲动，永远被某种新事物吸引；依我看，他对谁都谈不上特别的爱，然而他那普遍的善意与博爱，又远胜许多好恶之心强烈得多的人。他里面没有至圣所，没有一处可以偶尔接纳一两位选民进来、令人感到上帝临在的地方。他不是一座殿堂，而更像一栋舒适好客的宅邸，向所有朋友敞开，书籍与藏画陈设齐备，从阁楼到地窖，客人随意通行。关于两性关系，他抱持“开明的”见解。倒不是说他是个浪荡子，而是他不信婚姻，除非夫妻彼此仍是对方之必需。倘若一方觉得另一方乏味了，或觉得别人更有意味，他便认为理应分开。
这一切我不久便从他那里领教了，因为他谈吐全无保留。他对待侄女的方式很特别。他惯于当着她谈论各式各样的话题，因为他有一种理论：她应当受同男人一模一样的社交陶冶，男人知道什么，她也该知道什么。他言语从不粗鄙，但另一方面，他又当着我的面对她说出一些叫我脸上发烫的话。这样做的恶果会如何，我一时预见不到；但一个明显的好处是：在他家中，绝无那种可憎的、对女人降格以谈的做派；有女子在场，谈吐的层面绝不降低。
有一天，他谈起一部当时人人捧读的小说，我碰巧说，但愿写小说的人别再那样下笔，仿佛爱情就是人生存在的正中心和总和似的。一个男人的一生，除爱情之外还有那么多的内容；小说家们却百讲不厌，同一个故事翻来覆去，用一百种不同的形式讲个没完。
“我不同意你的话，”特蕾莎说，“我全然不同意。愚蠢空洞的感伤主义令我厌恶——叫我恶心；但我首先确信：一个男人，若不曾被——我本想说被吞噬——对某个女人的伟大挚恋所吞噬，便断然成不了任何气候。你们那些伟人的生平，一半是他们所崇敬的女人的历史，同他们本人的历史分毫不差地各占一半。但丁（Dante）、拜伦（Byron）、雪莱（Shelley），他们个个如此，这毫无差错；这是人生的重大事实。没有它，莎士比亚还算什么？而莎士比亚就是人生本身。一个配得上称之为人的人，直到步入老境，会发现爱情的事实永远迎面而来；倘若他不曾为世俗或放荡所毁，那么他五十岁时为之烦扰的程度，同二十五岁时一般无二。这是一切题目中的题目。人们咒骂爱情，把它当作世间一半祸患的根由。可它恰恰是使这世界保持端正的那唯一之物；若不是有那压倒一切的本能，人性便会分崩离析，沦入不可思议的自私与罪恶，最终落得普遍的自戕。我本不打算高谈雄辩：我憎恶高谈雄辩。可你刚才说的并不是你的真心；那话不过出自头脑，或者不过是唇齿之间说说而已。”

===Original===
Theresa’s little speech was delivered not with any heat of the blood. There was no excitement in her grey eyes, nor did her cheek burn. Her brain seemed to rule everything. This was an idea she had, and she kindled over it because it was an idea. It was impossible, of course, that she should say what she did without some movement of the organ in her breast, but how much share this organ had in her utterances I never could make out. How much was due to the interest which she as a looker-on felt in men and women, and how much was due to herself as a woman, was always a mystery to me.
She was fond of music, and occasionally I asked her to play to me. She had a great contempt for bungling, and not being a professional player, she never would try a piece in my presence of which she was not perfectly master. She particularly liked to play Mozart, and on my asking her once to play a piece of Beethoven, she turned round upon me and said: “You like Beethoven best. I knew you would. He encourages a luxurious revelling in the incomprehensible and indefinably sublime. He is not good for you.”
My work was so hard, and the hours were so long, that I had little or no time for reading, nor for thinking either, except so far as Wollaston and Theresa made me think. Wollaston himself took rather to science, although he was not scientific, and made a good deal of what he called psychology. He was not very profound, but he had picked up a few phrases, or if this word is too harsh, a few ideas about metaphysical matters from authors who contemned metaphysics, and with these he was perfectly satisfied. A stranger listening to him would at first consider him well read, but would soon be undeceived, and would find that these ideas were acquired long ago; that he had never gone behind or below them, and that they had never fructified in him, but were like hard stones, which he rattled in his pocket. He was totally unlike Mardon. Mardon, although he would have agreed with many of Wollaston’s results, differed entirely from him in the processes by which they had been brought about; and a mental comparison of the two often told me what I had been told over and over again, that what we believe is not of so much importance as the path by which we travel to it.
Theresa too, like her uncle, eschewed metaphysics, but she was a woman, and a woman’s impulses supplied in her the lack of those deeper questionings, and at times prompted them. She was far more original than he was, and was impatient of the narrowness of the circle in which he moved. Her love of music, for example, was a thing incomprehensible to him, and I do not remember that he ever sat for a quarter of an hour really listening to it. He would read the newspaper or do anything while she was playing. She never resented his inattention, except when he made a noise, and then, without any rebuke, she would break off and go away. This mode of treatment was the outcome of one of her theories. She disbelieved altogether in punishment, except when it was likely to do good, either to the person punished or to others. “A good deal of punishment,” she used to say, “is mere useless pain.”
Both Theresa and her uncle were kind and human, and I endeavoured to my utmost to repay them by working my hardest. My few hours of leisure were sweet, and when I spent them with Wollaston and Theresa, were interesting. I often asked myself why I found this mode of existence more tolerable than any other I had hitherto enjoyed. I had, it is true, an hour or two’s unspeakable peace in the early morning, but, as I have said, at nine my toil commenced, and, with a very brief interval for meals, lasted till seven. After seven I was too tired to do anything by myself, and could only keep awake if I happened to be in company.

===Chinese===
特蕾莎这一席话，说时并无半分血气之热。她灰色的眼睛里没有激动的神色，脸颊也不曾发烧。仿佛她的大脑统辖着一切。这是她怀抱的一个观念，她为它燃起，正因为它是一个观念。当然，她说了这些话，胸中那件器官不可能毫无悸动；但这器官在她的言辞里究竟占几分，我却始终摸不清。几分出自她作为旁观者对男女世人的兴味，几分出自她本人作为一个女人，这于我永远是个谜。
她喜爱音乐，我偶尔请她为我弹奏几曲。她极鄙视笨拙的手艺；她又不是职业琴师，凡未能烂熟于胸的曲子，绝不肯当着我的面试弹。她特别喜欢弹莫扎特（Mozart）；有一回我请她弹一支贝多芬（Beethoven），她猛地转过身来对我说：“您最喜欢的就是贝多芬。我早知道会这样。他纵容人穷奢极欲地沉醉于那不可理解、不可名状的崇高。他于您无益。”
我的活计如此繁重，工时又如此之长，以致我几乎没有读书的工夫，也没有思考的工夫——除非是沃拉斯顿和特蕾莎引我思考的那些。沃拉斯顿本人相当亲近科学，尽管算不上有科学头脑，还着实搬弄了一阵他所谓的“心理学”。他不很深刻，却拾得了几个词——这词若太刺耳，便说几个观念——都是从几位鄙薄形而上学的作者那里拾来的关于形而上事项的见解；有这些，他便心满意足了。陌生人听他谈吐，起初会当他博览群书，但很快便会醒悟，发现这些见解得来已久；他从不曾追溯到它们背后或底层，它们也从不曾在他心里开花结果，倒像几块硬石头，装在口袋里铿锵作响。他同马登毫无相似之处。马登虽会赞同沃拉斯顿的许多结论，达成结论的路径却同他全然两样；在心里把这两个人比较一番，常使我重新听见那被反复告诫过的话：我们所信的是什么，远不如我们循着怎样的路径抵达它来得要紧。
特蕾莎也像她伯父一样避形而上学而不谈；但她是个女人，女人的直觉冲动在她身上补足了那些更深追问的缺欠，有时还正是它们引出了追问。她远比他更有独创之才，也耐不得他所活动的那圈子的狭仄。譬如她对音乐的热爱，在他便是全然不可解的东西；我不记得他曾真正坐着听过一刻钟。她弹琴时，他总在看报，或者干别的什么。她从不计较他的心不在焉，除非他弄出声响；那时她也不加一句责备，只是中断琴曲，径自走开。这种处置方式出自她的一个理论。她对惩罚一概不信，除非它有望带来益处——无论于受罚者还是于旁人。“许多惩罚，”她常说，“纯粹是无用的痛苦。”
特蕾莎和她伯父都和善而有人情味，我也拼尽全力、以最勤勉的工作报答他们。我那寥寥几小时的闲暇是甜美的，若是同沃拉斯顿和特蕾莎一同度过，便还有趣味。我常自问，为何我觉得这种生活方式，比我此前享受过的任何一种都更可忍受。不错，清晨我确有一两个小时难以言喻的安宁；但如我所说，九点一到劳作便开始了，除去用餐的短短间歇，一直持续到七点。七点之后我已疲惫不堪，独自什么也做不成，除非恰好在人群中，才能勉强不睡去。

===Original===
One reason certainly why I was content, was Theresa herself. She was a constant study to me, and I could not for a long time obtain any consistent idea of her. She was not a this or a that or the other. She could not be summarily dismissed into any ordinary classification. At first I was sure she was hard, but I found by the merest accident that nearly all her earnings were given with utmost secrecy to support a couple of poor relatives. Then I thought her self-conscious, but this, when I came to think upon it, seemed a mere word. She was one of those women, and very rare they are, who deal in ideas, and reflectiveness must be self-conscious. At times she appeared passionless, so completely did her intellect dominate, and so superior was she to all the little arts and weaknesses of women; but this was a criticism she contradicted continually.
There was very little society at the Wollastons’, but occasionally a few friends called. One evening there was a little party, and the conversation flagged. Theresa said that it was a great mistake to bring people together with nothing special to do but talk. Nothing is more tedious than to be in a company assembled for no particular reason, and every host, if he asks more than two persons at the outside, ought to provide some entertainment. Talking is worth nothing unless it is perfectly spontaneous, and it cannot be spontaneous if there are sudden and blank silences, and nobody can think of a fresh departure. The master of the house is bound to do something. He ought to hire a Punch and Judy show, or get up a dance.
This spice of bitterness and flavour of rudeness was altogether characteristic of Theresa, and somebody resented it by reminding her that *she* was the hostess. “Of course,” she replied, “that is why I said it: what shall I do?” One of her gifts was memory, and her friends cried out at once that she should recite something. She hesitated a little, and then throwing herself back in her chair, began *The Lass of Lochroyan*. At first she was rather diffident, but she gathered strength as she went on. There is a passage in the middle of the poem in which Lord Gregory’s cruel mother pretends she is Lord Gregory, and refuses to recognise his former love, Annie of Lochroyan, as she stands outside his tower. The mother calls to Annie from the inside﻿—

> “Gin thou be Annie of Lochroyan
 

 (As I trow thou binna she),
 

 Now tell me some of the love tokèns
 

 That passed between thee and me.”
> “Oh, dinna ye mind, Lord Gregory,
 

 As we sat at the wine,
 

 We changed the rings frae our fingers,
 

 And I can show thee thine?
> “Oh, yours was gude, and gude enough,
 

 But aye the best was mine;
 

 For yours was o’ the gude red gowd,
 

 But mine o’ the diamond fine.”


===Chinese===
我之所以心安，一个原因固然是特蕾莎本人。她于我是门常读常新的功课，很长一段时间里，我都无法对她形成任何连贯一致的印象。她不是这一个，也不是那一个，更不是另一个。任何寻常的分类法都休想把她一言蔽之。起初我断定她冷硬，但纯属偶然，我发现她几乎全部的收入都在极端隐秘之中拿去供养两位穷亲戚了。后来我又觉得她顾影自鉴，但细想起来，这仿佛只是一句空话。她是那种以观念为业的女人——这样的女人极为罕见——而善于深思者，势必顾影自鉴。有时她显得全无激情，她的理智统摄得那样彻底，她又那样超然于女人的一切小小伎俩与软弱之上；可这一评判又不断被她自己推翻。
沃拉斯顿家里几乎没有什么社交，偶尔有三两朋友来访。某晚有个小小的聚会，谈兴渐滞。特蕾莎说，把一群人聚到一起，又没有特别的事可做，光让他们说话，是个大大的错误。再没有比莫名其妙地凑成一堆更乏味的了；做主人的，请客顶多以两位为限，若再多，便该备下些余兴。谈话若非全然自发自流，便一文不值；而倘若冷场骤至、一片空白，谁也想不出新的话头，谈话便无从自流。主人有责任做点什么。他该去雇一场潘趣和朱迪木偶戏（Punch and Judy show），或者张罗一场舞。
这一点苦涩之味、这一丝失礼之态，正是特蕾莎的全副本色；有人被触恼了，提醒她说，*她*才是今晚的女主人。“当然了，”她答道，“正因如此我才这么说：我该怎么办呢？”记忆是她的天赋之一，朋友们立刻叫嚷着要她背诵点什么。她略一踌躇，随即向椅背上一靠，诵起了《洛克罗扬的姑娘》（*The Lass of Lochroyan*）。起初她还有些拘谨，越诵越见气力。诗的中间有一段，写格雷戈里勋爵（Lord Gregory）狠心的母亲假扮成勋爵本人，不肯认他旧日的恋人——站在塔外的洛克罗扬的安妮（Annie of Lochroyan）。母亲从塔里向安妮喊道——

> “你若真是洛克罗扬的安妮
 

 （我可想你并不是她），
 

 那就说说你我之间
 

 互换了哪些情信物。”
> “哦，格雷戈里勋爵，你难道忘了，
 

 当年我们对坐饮酒，
 

 互相换下了手上的指环，
 

 你那枚如今就在我手中？
> “哦，你那枚是好的，也够好了，
 

 可最好的到底是我的那枚；
 

 因你那枚是足赤的红金，
 

 我那枚却是精美的钻石。”


===Original===
The last verse is as noble as anything in any ballad in the English language, and I thought that when Theresa was halfway through it her voice shook a good deal. There was a glass of flowers standing near her, and just as she came to an end her arm moved and the glass was in a moment on the floor, shivered into twenty pieces. I happened to be watching her, and felt perfectly sure that the movement of her arm was not accidental, and that her intention was to conceal, by the apparent mishap, an emotion which was increasing and becoming inconvenient. At any rate, if that was her object it was perfectly accomplished, for the recitation was abruptly terminated, there was general commiseration over the shattered vase, and when the pieces were picked up and order was restored, it was nearly time to separate.
Two of my chief failings were forgetfulness and a want of thoroughness in investigation. What misery have I not suffered from insufficient presentation of a case to myself, and from prompt conviction of insufficiency and inaccuracy by the person to whom I in turn presented it! What misery have I not suffered from the discovery that explicit directions to me had been overlooked or only half understood!
One day in particular, I had to take round a book to be “subscribed” which Wollaston had just published﻿—that is to say, I had to take a copy to each of the leading booksellers to see how many they would purchase. Some books are sold “thirteen as twelve,” the thirteenth book being given to the purchaser of twelve, and some are sold “twenty-five as twenty-four.” This book was to be sold “twenty-five as twenty-four,” according to Wollaston’s orders. I subscribed it thirteen as twelve. Wollaston was annoyed, as I could see, for I had to go over all my work again, but in accordance with his fixed principles, he was not out of temper.
It so happened that that same day he gave me some business correspondence which I was to look through; and having looked through it, I was to answer the last letter in the sense which he indicated. I read the correspondence and wrote the letter for his signature. As soon as he saw it, he pointed out to me that I had only half mastered the facts, and that my letter was all wrong. This greatly disturbed me, not only because I had vexed him and disappointed him, but because it was renewed evidence of my weakness. I thought that if I was incapable of getting to the bottom of such a very shallow complication as this, of what value were any of my thinkings on more difficult subjects, and I fell a prey to self-contempt and scepticism. Contempt from those about us is hard to bear, but God help the poor wretch who contemns himself.

===Chinese===
这最后一节，其高贵堪与英语语言中任何民谣的任何篇章相媲美；我注意到特蕾莎诵到一半时，声音抖得厉害。她近旁立着一只插花的玻璃杯，恰在她诵完的一刻，她的手臂一动，杯子霎时跌落在地，摔成了二十来片。我恰好在注视她，完全确信她手臂的动作绝非偶然，她的用意是借这表面的意外，掩住一种正在高涨、即将失态的情绪。无论如何，倘若这确是她的目的，那真是圆满达成：朗诵戛然而止，众人对碎瓶纷纷表示惋惜，等碎片拾起、秩序恢复，散会的时候也就差不多到了。
我的两大痼疾，一是健忘，二是探究时不求甚解。一件事，我先向自己交代得不够透彻，随后一经呈给别人，又被当即指出疏漏错谬——这中间我受过的苦，何可胜数！及至发现人家交代得明明白白的指示，竟被我忽略或只领会了一半——这中间我受过的苦，又何可胜数！
尤其有一回，我得带一部沃拉斯顿刚出版的书去四处“征订”——也就是说，把样书送到各家大书店，看他们肯进多少。有些书按“十二进十三”发售，即进十二册者加送一册；有些按“二十四进二十五”发售。按沃拉斯顿的吩咐，这部书该按“二十四进二十五”发售。我却按“十二进十三”征订了。我看得出沃拉斯顿很懊恼，因为我不得不把全部工作重来一遍；但依照他的固定原则，他并没有动气。
偏偏同一天，他交给我一批商务信函，要我通读一遍；读罢，再按他指示的意思回覆末一封信。我读了信函，替他拟好回信待签。他一看之下便向我指出：事实我只掌握了一半，信写得全然不对。这使我大为不安，不仅因为我惹恼了他、辜负了他，更因为这是我软弱无能在眼前又添的新证。我想，倘若连这样一桩浅薄至极的纠葛，我都无力穷其底蕴，那么我对更艰深题目的种种思考，还有什么价值可言？于是我沦为自我鄙夷与怀疑主义的猎物。旁人的轻蔑已难忍受，但那个轻蔑自己的可怜虫，愿上帝救他。

===Original===
How well I recollect the early walk on the following morning in Kensington Gardens, the feeling of my own utter worthlessness, and the longing for death as the cancellation of the blunder of my existence! I went home, and after breakfast some proofs came from the printer of a pamphlet which Wollaston had in hand. Without unfastening them, he gave them to me, and said that as he had no time to read them himself, I must go upstairs to Theresa’s study and read them off with her. Accordingly I went and began to read. She took the manuscript and I took the proof. She read about a page, and then she suddenly stopped. “Oh, Mr. Rutherford,” she said, it, “what have you done? I heard my uncle distinctly tell you to mark on the manuscript when it went to the printer, that it was to be printed in demy octavo, and you have marked it twelvemo.”
I had had little sleep that night, I was exhausted with my early walk, and suddenly the room seemed to fade from me and I fainted. When I came to myself, I found that Theresa had not sought for any help; she had done all that ought to be done. She had unfastened my collar and had sponged my face with cold water. The first thing I saw as I gradually recovered myself, was her eyes looking steadily at me as she stood over me, and I felt her hand upon my head. When she was sure I was coming to myself, she held off and sat down in her chair.
I was a little hysterical, and after the fit was over I broke loose. With a storm of tears, I laid open all my heart. I told her how nothing I had ever attempted had succeeded; that I had never even been able to attain that degree of satisfaction with myself and my own conclusions, without which a man cannot live; and that now I found I was useless, even to the best friends I had ever known, and that the meanest clerk in the city would serve them better than I did. I was beside myself, and I threw myself on my knees, burying my face in Theresa’s lap and sobbing convulsively. She did not repel me, but she gently passed her fingers through my hair. Oh, the transport of that touch! It was as if water had been poured on a burnt hand, or some miraculous Messiah had soothed the delirium of a fever-stricken sufferer, and replaced his visions of torment with dreams of Paradise.
She gently lifted me up, and as I rose I saw her eyes too were wet. “My poor friend,” she said, “I cannot talk to you now. You are not strong enough, and for that matter, nor am I, but let me say this to you, that you are altogether mistaken about yourself. The meanest clerk in the city could not take your place here.” There was just a slight emphasis I thought upon the word “here.” “Now,” she said, “you had better go. I will see about the pamphlet.”
I went out mechanically, and I anticipate my story so far as to say that, two days after, another proof came in the proper form. I went to the printer to offer to pay for setting it up afresh, and was told that Miss Wollaston had been there and had paid herself for the rectification of the mistake, giving special injunctions that no notice of it was to be given to her uncle. I should like to add one more beatitude to those of the gospels and to say, Blessed are they who heal us of self-despisings. Of all services which can be done to man, I know of none more precious.

===Chinese===
次日清晨在肯辛顿花园里散步的情形，我记得多么清楚：那种自觉一无是处的感觉，那种渴望一死、好注销我此生这场大错的心情！我回到家里，早饭后，印刷所送来了沃拉斯顿手头一部小册子的几页校样。他解也没有解，就把校样交给我，说自己无暇过目，我必须上楼到特蕾莎的书房，同她一起把校样读一遍。我便上去，开始诵读。她执原稿，我执校样。她念了约莫一页，突然停住了。“哦，拉瑟福德先生，”她说，“您这是干了什么呀？我清清楚楚听见伯父吩咐过您，稿件付印时要在稿面上注明按中等八开本印，您却标成了十二开本。”
头天夜里我本就没睡多少，清晨的散步又使我筋疲力尽；忽然间，房间仿佛从我眼前淡去，我晕了过去。等我苏醒过来，发觉特蕾莎并没有去喊人帮忙；该做的一切，她都已做了。她解开了我的衣领，用冷水为我擦了脸。当我渐渐恢复知觉，最先看见的，是她俯身立在我身旁、静静注视我的那双眼睛；我还觉出她的手正放在我头上。等她确信我已回转过来，便退开身子，坐回自己的椅子上。
我有些歇斯底里，那一阵过去之后，我便再也把持不住。伴着一场泪雨，我把心底的一切倾倒出来。我告诉她，我一向所尝试的事没有一件成了功；我甚至始终未能达到那种对自己、对自己的结论的满足——没有它，人是活不下去的；如今我又发现自己百无一用，即便对平生所遇最好的朋友也是如此，伦敦城里最低微的书记员，都比我更合他们的用。我忘乎所以，扑跪在地，把脸埋进特蕾莎的膝头，抽抽噎噎地恸哭起来。她没有推开我，只把手指轻轻插进我的头发。哦，那一触带来的狂喜！仿佛清水浇上了灼伤的手掌，仿佛某位行奇迹的弥赛亚（Messiah）抚慰了热病者的谵妄，将他的种种苦刑幻象，换作了乐园的梦境。
她轻轻把我扶起来；起身时，我看见她的眼睛也湿润了。“我可怜的朋友，”她说，“现在我不能同你细谈。你的力气撑不住，说起来，我也撑不住。但容我对你说一句：你对自己全然误会了。城里最低微的书记员，也顶替不了你在这儿的位子。”我觉出“这儿”两个字上恰好带着一点轻微的重音。“好了，”她说，“您最好去吧。小册子的事有我呢。”
我木然走了出去；这里且把故事往前提一提：两天之后，另一份规格无误的校样送来了。我去印刷所，提出由我付重排的费用，却被告知沃拉斯顿小姐已经来过，自己掏钱付清了改正错误的费用，还特别叮嘱，万万不可让她伯父知晓此事。我很愿在福音书那诸般福辞之外再添上一条，说：医治我们自轻之病的人有福了。在一切可为人类效劳的事里，我不知道还有比这更珍贵的。

===Original===
When I went back to my work I worshipped Theresa, and was entirely overcome with unhesitating, absorbing love for her. I saw nothing more of her that day nor the next day. Her uncle told me that she had gone into the country, and that probably she would not return for some time, as she had purposed paying a lengthened visit to a friend at a distance. I had a mind to write to her; but I felt as I have often felt before in great crises, a restraint which was gentle and incomprehensible, but nevertheless unmistakable. I suppose it is not what would be called conscience, as conscience is supposed to decide solely between right and wrong, but it was none the less peremptory, although its voice was so soft and low that it might easily have been overlooked. Over and over again, when I have purposed doing a thing, have I been impeded or arrested by this same silent monitor, and never have I known its warnings to be the mere false alarms of fancy.
After a time, the thought of Mary recurred to me. I was distressed to find that, in the very height of my love for Theresa, my love for Mary continued unabated. Had it been otherwise, had my affection for Mary grown dim, I should not have been so much perplexed, but it did not. It may be ignominious to confess it, but so it was; I simply record the fact.
I had not seen Mardon since that last memorable evening at his house, but one day as I was sitting in the shop, who should walk in but Mary herself. The meeting, although strange, was easily explained. Her father was ill, and could do nothing but read. Wollaston published freethinking books, and Mardon had noticed in an advertisement the name of a book which he particularly wished to see. Accordingly he sent Mary for it. She pressed me very much to call on him. He had talked about me a good deal, and had written to me at the last address he knew, but the letter had been returned through the dead-letter office.
It was a week before I could go, and when I did go, I found him much worse than I had imagined him to be. There was no virulent disease of any particular organ, but he was slowly wasting away from atrophy, and he knew, or thought he knew, he should not recover. But he was perfectly self-possessed.
“With regard to immortality,” he said, “I never know what men mean by it. *What* self is it which is to be immortal? Is it really desired by anybody that he should continue to exist forever with his present limitations and failings? Yet if these are not continued, the man does not continue, but something else, a totally different person. I believe in the survival of life and thought. People think is not enough. They say they want the survival of their personality. It is very difficult to express any conjecture upon the matter, especially now when I am weak, and I have no system﻿—nothing but surmises. One thing I am sure of﻿—that a man ought to rid himself as much as possible of the miserable egotism which is so anxious about self, and should be more and more anxious about the Universal.”

===Chinese===
回到工作岗位后，我崇拜着特蕾莎，全然被一种毫不犹豫、吞没一切的爱所征服。那一日和次日，我都再没见到她。她伯父告诉我，她到乡下去了，一时多半不会回来，因为她打算到远处一位朋友家作长期拜访。我想过给她写信；可我感到——从前每逢重大关头，我常这样感到——一种温存而不可解、却又明白无误的约束。我想这算不上通常所谓的良知，因为良知照理只在善恶之间裁断；但这约束并不因此稍减其令行禁止之力，尽管它的声音那样轻、那样低，低得很容易被人忽略。一次又一次，当我决意做一件事时，总被这同一位沉默的谏止者拦阻或截停；而它的警告，我从不曾发现哪一回纯属想象的虚惊。
过了一阵，玛丽的影子又回到我心头。我发现，正当我对特蕾莎的爱恋攀至顶点时，我对玛丽的爱竟丝毫不曾消减，这使我深感苦恼。倘若不是这样，倘若我对玛丽的情意已然黯淡，我还不至于这样进退两难，可它偏偏不曾。承认这一点也许有失体面，然而事实如此；我只是照实记下。
自他家中那个难忘的夜晚之后，我再没见过马登；可是有一天，我正坐在铺子里，走进来的不是别人，正是玛丽。这次会面虽显突兀，解释起来却容易。她父亲病了，成天只能以读书消遣。沃拉斯顿出版自由思想的书，马登在一则广告里瞥见一部书名，正很想一见，便差玛丽来买。她再三劝我去探望他。他常谈起我，还往他所知的我最后一个地址写过信，那信却经死信处退了回去。
过了一星期我才得以成行；去后才发现，他比我想象的还要糟糕得多。并没有哪个器官染着凶险的恶疾，他只是患了萎损之症，一日一日缓缓地消瘦下去；他自己知道——或者自以为知道——再也起不来了。但他神色泰然，镇定自若。
“至于不朽，”他说，“我从来不明白人们说它是什么意思。究竟是怎样的一个*自我*要去不朽？真有人愿意带着他现在的局限与缺失，永远继续存在下去吗？可这些若不随之延续，那人便没有延续，延续的是别的东西，一个截然不同的人。我相信生命与思想的存活。人们觉得这还不够。他们说要的是他们人格的存活。在这个问题上发表任何猜想都极为困难，尤其此刻我又虚弱；我没有什么体系——只有些揣测。有一件事我确信不疑：人应当尽可能摆脱那种斤斤于自我的可怜的自我主义，应当越来越挂怀那普遍者。”

===Original===
Mardon grew slowly worse. The winter was coming on, and as the temperature fell and the days grew darker, he declined. With all his heroism and hardness he had a weakness or two, and one was, that he did not want to die in London or be buried there. So we got him down to Sandgate near Hythe, and procured lodging for him close to the sea, so that he could lie in bed and watch the sun and moon rise over the water. Mary, of course, remained with him, and I returned to London.
Towards the end of November I got a letter, to tell me that if I wished to see him alive again, I must go down at once. I went that day, and I found that the doctor had been and had said that before the morning the end must come. Mardon was perfectly conscious, in no pain, and quite calm. He was just able to speak. When I went into his bedroom, he smiled, and without any preface or introduction he said: “Learn not to be overanxious about meeting troubles and solving difficulties which time will meet and solve for you.” Excepting to ask for water, I don’t think he spoke again.
All that night Mary and I watched in that topmost garret looking out over the ocean. It was a night entirely unclouded, and the moon was at the full. Towards daybreak her father moaned a little, then became quite quiet, and just as the dawn was changing to sunrise, he passed away. What a sunrise it was! For about half-an-hour before the sun actually appeared, the perfectly smooth water was one mass of gently heaving opaline lustre. Not a sound was to be heard, and over in the southeast hung the planet Venus. Death was in the chamber, but the surpassing splendour of the pageant outside arrested us, and we sat awed and silent. Not till the first burning-point of the great orb itself emerged above the horizon, not till the day awoke with its brightness and brought with it the sounds of the day and its cares, did we give way to our grief.
It was impossible for me to stay. It was not that I was obliged to get back to my work in London, but I felt that Mary would far rather be alone, and that it would not be proper for me to remain. The woman of the house in which the lodgings were was very kind, and promised to do all that was necessary. It was arranged that I should come down again to the funeral.
So I went back to London. Before I had got twenty miles on my journey the glory of a few hours had turned into autumn storm. The rain came down in torrents, and the wind rushed across the country in great blasts, stripping the trees, and driving over the sky with hurricane speed great masses of continuous cloud, which mingled earth and heaven. I thought of all the ships which were on the sea in the night, sailing under the serene stars which I had seen rise and set; I thought of Mardon lying dead, and I thought of Mary. The simultaneous passage through great emotions welds souls, and begets the strongest of all forms of love. Those who have sobbed together over a dead friend, who have held one another’s hands in that dread hour, feel a bond of sympathy, pure and sacred, which nothing can dissolve.

===Chinese===
马登的病势缓慢地沉了下去。冬天将临，气温日降，昼色日暗，他也随之衰减。他尽管刚毅坚强，却也有一两处软弱，其中之一便是：他不愿死在伦敦，也不愿葬在伦敦。于是我们送他南下到海斯（Hythe）附近的桑德盖特（Sandgate），在紧邻海边的地方替他觅了住处，让他能躺在床上，望日月从水上升起。玛丽自然留在他身边，我便回了伦敦。
十一月末，我收到一封信，说倘若我还想见他一面活人，必须立刻南下。我当天就去了；到时医生已经来过，说是天亮之前，大限必至。马登神志完全清明，不觉疼痛，安详平静，还勉强能够说话。我走进他的卧房，他微微一笑，开门见山地说：“对那些时间自会替你迎受、替你化解的烦恼与难题，要学会不要过分焦心。”此后除了讨水喝，我想他再没说过一句话。
那一夜，玛丽和我就在那间俯瞰大海的顶层阁楼里守夜。那晚万里无云，月轮正满。将近破晓，她父亲低哼了几声，随即全然安静下去；恰在晨光将转为日出的一刻，他溘然长逝了。那是怎样的一场日出啊！太阳真正露面之前约莫半个钟头里，平滑如镜的海面整个成了一片微微起伏的乳白珠光。四下悄然无声，东南方向悬着金星。死亡就在屋里，可窗外那场无与伦比的壮丽攫住了我们；我们坐着，肃然无言。直到那巨轮本身第一点烧红的锋芒冒出地平线，直到白昼携着它的光亮醒来、带来白昼的声息与操劳，我们才放声恸哭。
我不便逗留。倒不是伦敦的活计非我回去不可，而是我感到玛丽宁愿独自一人，我留在那里也不合适。寄宿那家的女主人极为和善，应承下一切必需之事。我们商定，下葬之日我再来。
我便回了伦敦。上路未及二十英里，几个时辰之前的荣光已化作秋日的风暴。大雨倾盆而下，狂风一阵阵扫过原野，剥尽树上的残叶，又驱赶着连绵的巨大云团以飓风之速碾过天空，天地浑然混作一色。我想起夜间还行驶在海上的一切船只，它们航行在我曾看其升落的那宁静星辰之下；我想起已然长逝的马登，又想起玛丽。同一时刻共历巨大的情感，会将灵魂熔铸在一起，孕育出一切形态的爱之中最强烈的那种。曾对着一位亡友一同啜泣、在那可畏的时刻彼此执手的人，他们之间系着一条纯真而神圣的同情之纽，再没有什么能将它解开了。

===Original===
I went to the funeral as appointed. There was some little difficulty about it, for Mary, who knew her father so well, was unconquerably reluctant that an inconsistency should crown the career of one who, all through life, had been so completely self-accordant. She could not bear that he should be buried with a ceremony which he despised, and she was altogether free from that weakness which induces a compliance with the rites of the Church from persons who avow themselves sceptics.
At last a burying-ground was found, belonging to a little half-forsaken Unitarian chapel; and there Mardon was laid. A few friends came from London, one of whom had been a Unitarian minister, and he “conducted the service,” such as it was. It was of the simplest kind. The body was taken to the side of the grave, and before it was lowered a few words were said, calling to mind all the virtues of him whom we had lost. These the speaker presented to us with much power and sympathy. He did not merely catalogue a disconnected string of excellences, but he seemed to plant himself in the central point of Mardon’s nature, and to see from what it radiated.
He then passed on to say that about immortality, as usually understood, he knew nothing; but that Mardon would live as every force in nature lives﻿—forever; transmuted into a thousand different forms; the original form utterly forgotten, but never perishing. The cloud breaks up and comes down upon the earth in showers which cease, but the clouds and the showers are really undying. This may be true﻿—but, after all, I can only accept the fact of death in silence, as we accept the loss of youth and all other calamities. We are able to see that the arrangements which we should make, if we had the control of the universe, would be more absurd than those which prevail now. We are able to see that an eternity of life in one particular form, with one particular set of relationships, would be misery to many and mischievous to everybody, however sweet those relationships may be to some of us. At times we are reconciled to death as the great regenerator, and we pine for escape from the surroundings of which we have grown weary; but we can say no more, and the hour of illumination has not yet come. Whether it ever will come to a more nobly developed race we cannot tell.
---
Thus far goes the manuscript which I have in my possession. I know that there is more of it, but all my search for it has been in vain. Possibly some day I may be able to recover it. My friend discontinued his notes for some years, and consequently the concluding portion of them was entirely separate from the earlier portion, and this is the reason, I suppose, why it is missing.

===Chinese===
我如期前去送葬。这事颇费了些周折：玛丽深知父亲为人，无论如何也不肯让一桩自相矛盾之事，为一世言行如一的人生加冕收梢。她不能容忍他受一套他素来鄙夷的仪式安葬；她也全然没有那种软弱——自认怀疑论者，却又顺从教会仪文的那种软弱。
最后寻到一处墓地，属一座半被废弃的小型一位论礼拜堂所有；马登便安葬在那里。几位朋友从伦敦赶来，其中一位曾做过一位论牧师，由他“主持了仪式”——姑且称之为仪式。其简单至极。遗体被送到墓穴之侧，落葬之前，有人讲了寥寥数语，追念我们所失去的这个人的种种德行。致辞者讲述这些德行时，既有力量，又富同情。他并未只是罗列一串互不相连的美德，倒像是把自己安放在马登天性的正中心，看清了这一切由何辐射而出。
接着他又说，至于照通常理解的不朽，他一无所知；但马登会活着，一如自然界中每一种力都活着——永远活着；转化为一千种不同的形态；原本的形态被彻底遗忘，却从不消亡。云破碎开来，化作阵雨落于大地，雨会停歇，可云与雨其实永不消亡。这或许是真的——但归根结底，对于死亡这一事实，我只能默然接受，一如我们接受青春的消逝和其他一切灾祸。我们看得出来，倘若宇宙由我们执掌，我们所作的安排，会比现行的一切更加荒谬。我们看得出来，永远以某一种特定形态、某一组特定的关系活下去，对许多人将是苦难，对所有人都将为害——无论那些关系在我们某些人眼中何等甘美。有时我们与死亡和解，把它当作伟大的再造者，渴望逃离那些我们已然厌倦的环境；但我们能说的仅止于此，豁然贯通的时刻尚未到来。它究竟会不会降临于一个发展得更为高贵的族类，我们无从知晓。
---
我手中这部手稿，到此为止。我知道它后面还有，但千方百计寻访，始终徒劳。或许有朝一日，我还能把它觅回。我的朋友曾中断笔记数年，因此笔记的末后部分同前面部分原本便是分开存放的；我想，这便是它散失的缘由。

===Original===
Miss Mardon soon followed her father. She caught cold at his funeral; the seeds of consumption developed themselves with remarkable rapidity, and in less than a month she had gone. Her father’s peculiar habits had greatly isolated him, and Miss Mardon had scarcely any friends. Rutherford went to see her continually, and during the last few nights sat up with her, incurring not a little scandal and gossip, to which he was entirely insensible.
For a time he was utterly brokenhearted; and not only brokenhearted, but broken-spirited, and incapable of attacking the least difficulty. All the springs of his nature were softened, so that if anything was cast upon him, there it remained without hope, and without any effort being made to remove it. He only began to recover when he was forced to give up work altogether and take a long holiday. To do this he was obliged to leave Mr. Wollaston, and the means of obtaining his much-needed rest were afforded him, partly by what he had saved, and partly by the kindness of one or two whom he had known.
I thought that Miss Mardon’s death would permanently increase my friend’s intellectual despondency, but it did not. On the contrary, he gradually grew out of it. A crisis seemed to take a turn just then, and he became less involved in his old speculations, and more devoted to other pursuits. I fancy that something happened; there was some word revealed to him, or there was some recoil, some healthy horror of eclipse in this self-created gloom which drove him out of it.

===Chinese===
不久，马登小姐便随她父亲去了。她是在葬礼上染的风寒；痨病的种子以惊人的速度发作起来，不出一个月，她便故去了。她父亲的特异习性使他们与世隔绝，马登小姐几乎没有什么朋友。拉瑟福德不断前去探望，最后几夜更守在她床侧，招来了不少物议与闲话，而他全然无所觉。
有一段时日，他伤心欲绝；不仅是伤心，而且心气尽丧，连最微小的困难也无力应付。他天性中的一切弹簧都疲软了，无论什么落到他身上，便原样压在那里，没有指望，也没有人设法把它挪开。直到他被迫抛开一切工作、作一次长期休养，他才渐渐复原。为此他不得不离开沃拉斯顿先生；他亟需的静养之资，一部分来自自己的积蓄，一部分来自一两位故人的善意。
我原以为马登小姐之死，会叫我朋友智识上的消沉永久加深，事实却不然。恰恰相反，他渐渐从中长了出来。危机仿佛恰在那时转了向：他对旧日玄思的纠缠渐少，对其他事务的用心渐多。我揣想总有事情发生了；或有某个字向他显明，或是起了某种反挫——某种健康的惊觉，惊觉自己将在这自造的阴郁中湮没——把他从中驱赶了出来。

===Original===
He accidentally renewed his acquaintance with the butterfly-catcher, who was obliged to leave the country and come up to London. He, however, did not give up his old hobby, and the two friends used every Sunday in summer time to sally forth some distance from town and spend the whole livelong day upon the downs and in the green lanes of Surrey. Both of them had to work hard during the week. Rutherford, who had learned shorthand when he was young, got employment upon a newspaper, and ultimately a seat in the gallery of the House of Commons. He never took to collecting insects like his companion, nor indeed to any scientific pursuits, but he certainly changed.
I find it very difficult to describe exactly what the change was, because it was into nothing positive; into no sect, party, nor special mode. He did not, for example, go off into absolute denial. I remember his telling me, that to suppress speculation would be a violence done to our nature as unnatural as if we were to prohibit ourselves from looking up to the blue depths between the stars at night; as if we were to determine that nature required correcting in this respect, and that we ought to be so constructed as not to be able to see anything but the earth and what lies on it. Still, these things in a measure ceased to worry him, and the long conflict died away gradually into a peace not formally concluded, and with no specific stipulations, but nevertheless definite. He was content to rest and wait. Better health and time, which does so much for us, brought this about. The passage of years gradually relaxed his anxiety about death by loosening his anxiety for life without loosening his love of life.
But I would rather not go into any further details, because I still cherish the hope that some day or the other I may recover the contents of the diary. I am afraid that up to this point he has misrepresented himself, and that those who read his story will think him nothing but a mere egoist, selfish and self-absorbed. Morbid he may have been, but selfish he was not. A more perfect friend I never knew, nor one more capable of complete abandonment to a person for whom he had any real regard, and I can only hope that it may be my good fortune to find the materials which will enable me to represent him autobiographically in a somewhat different light to that in which he appears now.

===Chinese===
他偶然重逢了那位捕蝶人——那人不得不离开乡下，来到伦敦。他却并未丢掉旧日的癖好；夏季里每个礼拜天，两位朋友总要出城走上一程，在萨里郡（Surrey）的丘原和青葱的巷陌间消磨整整一天。两人在周中都得苦干。拉瑟福德年轻时学过速记，在一家报馆谋得职位，最终在众议院（House of Commons）记者席上赢得了一个席位。他从不曾像他同伴那样迷上采集昆虫，也实在不曾迷上任何科学消遣，但他确乎变了。
要确切说出这变化究竟为何，我觉得很难，因为它并非变成任何确定的东西；不曾变成任何宗派、党派，或特殊的门径。譬如说，他并不曾转入绝对的否定。我记得他对我说过，压制玄思乃是对我们天性的一种强暴，其不自然，一如禁止自己仰望夜间星辰之间那蓝色的深处；一如断定天性在此一端需要修正，断定我们理当被造得只能看见大地和地上之物，此外一概不见。然而，这些事毕竟在一定程度上不再搅扰他了；那场漫长的争斗渐渐消歇，化为一种并非正式缔结、亦无具体条款、却终究明确无疑的和平。他安于休息，安于等待。较健的身体，加上时间——时间替我们做的事何其多——促成了这一切。岁月的推移，松开了他对生命的焦灼，从而也松开了他对死亡的焦虑，却不曾松开他对生命的热爱。
但其余细节我不愿再往下写了，因为我仍怀着希望：有朝一日，或许还能把日记的其余内容寻回。我担心行文至此，他把自己描摹走了样，读他故事的人会当他不过是个自我主义者，自私而已，顾影自怜而已。他或许病态，却绝不自私。更完美的朋友，我平生未遇；也再没有谁，能像他那样，对任何一个他真心看重的人倾付自己的全部。我只能指望自己有此幸运，寻得那些材料，好让他以自传的面目，显出与现在这副形象略有不同的另一种光景。

## Preface to the Second Edition / 第二版序

===Original===
The present edition is a reprint of the first, with corrections of several mistakes which had been overlooked.
There is one observation which I may perhaps be permitted to make on rereading after some years this autobiography. Rutherford, at any rate in his earlier life, was an example of the danger and the folly of cultivating thoughts and reading books to which he was not equal, and which tend to make a man lonely.
It is all very well that remarkable persons should occupy themselves with exalted subjects, which are out of the ordinary road which ordinary humanity treads; but we who are not remarkable make a very great mistake if we have anything to do with them. If we wish to be happy, and have to live with average men and women, as most of us have to live, we must learn to take an interest in the topics which concern average men and women. We think too much of ourselves. We ought not to sacrifice a single moment’s pleasure in our attempt to do something which is too big for us, and as a rule, men and women are always attempting what is too big for them. To ninety-nine young men out of a hundred, or perhaps ninety-nine thousand nine hundred and ninety-nine out of a hundred thousand, the wholesome healthy doctrine is, “Don’t bother yourselves with what is beyond you; try to lead a sweet, clean, wholesome life, keep yourselves in health above everything, stick to your work, and when your day is done amuse and refresh yourselves.”
It is not only a duty to ourselves, but it is a duty to others to take this course. Great men do the world much good, but not without some harm, and we have no business to be troubling ourselves with their dreams if we have duties which lie nearer home amongst persons to whom these dreams are incomprehensible. Many a man goes into his study, shuts himself up with his poetry or his psychology, comes out, half understanding what he has read, is miserable because he cannot find anybody with whom he can talk about it, and misses altogether the far more genuine joy which he could have obtained from a game with his children or listening to what his wife had to tell him about her neighbours.

===Chinese===
本版系第一版之重印，并订正了若干此前失于校核的错误。
时隔数年重读这部自传，有一点评议，或许容我置喙。至少就拉瑟福德的早年而言，他正是一个例证，说明沉溺于自己力所不逮的思想、阅读自己力不能胜的书籍，是何等危险而又愚蠢，因为这些皆足以使人落落寡合。
杰出之人尽可潜心于崇高的题旨——那些题旨本在寻常世人所走的寻常道路之外；但我们这些并不杰出的人，倘若同它们有半点瓜葛，便铸成大错了。我们若想快乐，又不得不同寻常男女相处——我们多数人注定要这样生活——就必须学着去关心寻常男女所关心的话题。我们把自己看得太重。我们不该为做那些于我们过于庞巨之事而牺牲哪怕片刻的欢愉；而照例说来，世人所尝试的，又无不是对他们而言过于庞巨之事。对一百个青年中的九十九个，或者十万中的九万九千九百九十九个而言，有益身心的箴言是：“不要为力所不及的事自寻烦恼；去过一种甜美、洁净、健康的生活，把身体看得重于一切，守好你的本行，一天的事做完，便消遣消遣，歇息歇息。”
行此之道，不仅是对于自己的本分，也是对于他人的本分。伟人于世界多有裨益，却并非不带来几分损害；倘若我们的本分就在近旁，在于那些对这些梦想全然无从索解的人们中间，我们便无权为他们的梦想自扰。多少人走进书房，同他的诗歌或心理学闭门独处，出来时对自己所读一知半解，因找不到一个可以谈论的人而郁郁寡欢，却把本可从陪儿女玩一场游戏、或听妻子讲讲左邻右舍中得来的远为真实的快乐，整个儿错过了。

===Original===
“Lor, miss, you haven’t looked at your new bonnet today,” said a servant girl to her young mistress.
“No, why should I? I did not want to go out.”
“Oh, how can you? why, I get mine out and look at it every night.”
She was happy for a whole fortnight with a happiness cheap at a very high price.
That same young mistress was very caustic upon the women who block the pavement outside drapers’ shops, but surely she was unjust. They always seem unconscious, to be enjoying themselves intensely and most innocently, more so probably than an audience at a Wagner concert. Many persons with refined minds are apt to depreciate happiness, especially if it is of “a low type.” Broadly speaking, it is the one thing worth having, and low or high, if it does no mischief, is better than the most spiritual misery.

===Chinese===
“哎呀，小姐，您今天还没瞧过您那顶新女帽呢。”一个小女仆对她的年轻女主人说。
“没瞧过，干吗要瞧？我又不想出门。”
“哦，您怎么能这样？我可是每天晚上都要把我那顶取出来瞧一瞧呢。”
她为此足足快乐了两个星期——这份快乐，纵然标出天价，也算便宜。
而那位年轻女主人，对那些把绸布店门外人行道挤得水泄不通的女人们言辞刻薄，但她想必有失公允。她们看上去总是浑然不觉，尽兴而又极清白地享受着，那份快乐说不定比瓦格纳（Wagner）音乐会上的听众还要多些。许多心智雅致的人动辄贬抑快乐，倘若那快乐属于“低级类型”，便尤其如此。笼统地说，快乐是唯一值得拥有的东西；无论低级高级，只要不为害于人，便胜过最属灵的痛苦。

===Original===
Metaphysics and theology, including all speculations on the why and the wherefore, optimism, pessimism, freedom, necessity, causality, and so forth, are not only for the most part loss of time, but frequently ruinous. It is no answer to say that these things force themselves upon us, and that to every question we are bound to give or try to give an answer. It is true, although strange, that there are multitudes of burning questions which we must do our best to ignore, to forget their existence; and it is not more strange, after all, than many other facts in this wonderfully mysterious and defective existence of ours. One fourth of life is intelligible, the other three-fourths is unintelligible darkness; and our earliest duty is to cultivate the habit of not looking round the corner.
“Go thy way, eat thy bread with joy, and drink thy wine with a merry heart; for God hath already accepted thy works. Let thy garments be always white, and let not thy head lack ointment. Live joyfully with the wife whom thou lovest all the days of the life of thy vanity, which He hath given thee under the sun, all the days of thy vanity: for that is thy portion in life.”
R. S.

===Chinese===
形而上学与神学，连同一切关于所以然与何以然的玄思——乐观主义、悲观主义、自由、必然、因果等等——不仅大抵是虚耗光阴，而且往往祸患无穷。若说这些问题会不请自来，说每一个问题我们都必须给出或试图给出答案，那算不得答辩。事情虽然奇怪，却是真的：世上有无数灼手的问题，我们必须竭力置之不理，竭力忘掉它们的存在；而归根结底，这并不比我们这段奇妙、神秘而处处有缺陷的存在中的其他许多事实更为奇怪。人生有四分之一可以理喻，其余四分之三则是不可理喻的黑暗；而我们最早的本分，便是养成一种习惯：不向墙角后面张望。
“你只管去欢欢喜喜吃你的饭，心中快乐喝你的酒，因为神已经悦纳你的作为。你的衣服当时常洁白，你头上也不要缺少膏油。在你一生虚空的年日，就是他在日光之下赐你的虚空年日，当同你所爱的妻快活度日，因为那是你在一生中所得的分。”
R. S.

## II Preparation / 第二章 预备

===Original===
It was necessary that an occupation should be found for me, and after much deliberation it was settled that I should “go into the ministry.” I had joined the church, I had “engaged in prayer” publicly, and although I had not set up for being extraordinarily pious, I was thought to be as good as most of the young men who professed to have a mission to regenerate mankind.

Accordingly, after some months of preparation, I was taken to a Dissenting College not very far from where we lived. It was a large old-fashioned house with a newer building annexed, and was surrounded with a garden and with meadows. Each student had a separate room, and all had their meals together in a common hall. Altogether there were about forty of us. The establishment consisted of a President, an elderly gentleman who had an American degree of doctor of divinity, and who taught the various branches of theology. He was assisted by three professors, who imparted to us as much Greek, Latin, and mathematics as it was considered that we ought to know. Behold me, then, beginning a course of training which was to prepare me to meet the doubts of the nineteenth century; to be the guide of men; to advise them in their perplexities; to suppress their tempestuous lusts; to lift them above their petty cares, and to lead them heavenward!

About the Greek and Latin and the secular part of the college discipline I will say nothing, except that it was generally inefficient. The theological and Biblical teaching was a sham. We had come to the college in the first place to learn the Bible. Our whole existence was in future to be based upon that book; our lives were to be passed in preaching it. I will venture to say that there was no book less understood either by students or professors. The President had a course of lectures, delivered year after year to successive generations of his pupils, upon its authenticity and inspiration. They were altogether remote from the subject; and afterwards, when I came to know what the difficulties of belief really were, I found that these essays, which were supposed to be a triumphant confutation of the sceptic, were a mere sword of lath. They never touched the question, and if any doubts suggested themselves to the audience, nobody dared to give them tongue, lest the expression of them should beget a suspicion of heresy.

===Chinese===
总得替我找一个营生；再三斟酌之后，事情定下来了：我该「投身传道事工」。我已经加入了教会，也当众「参与祷告」过；虽说我不曾以格外虔诚自居，人们却认为，比起大多数自称负有革新人类之使命的青年来，我并不逊色。

于是，经过几个月的准备，我被送到离我们家不远的一所不奉国教派学院（Dissenting College）。那是一座老式的大宅子，旁边接出一幢较新的楼，四周环绕着花园和草场。每个学生有一间单人房，众人在公共饭厅里一起用膳。我们总共约四十人。院里有一位院长，是一位持有美国神学博士学位的年长绅士，讲授神学的各个分支。协助他的有三位教授，把希腊文、拉丁文和数学传授给我们——传授到人们认为我们应当知道的程度为止。看哪，我便这样开始了一门训练课程，它要装备我去迎战十九世纪的种种怀疑；去做世人的向导；在他们困顿之时为他们出谋；压制他们狂暴的情欲；把他们从小小的忧烦中超拔出来，领他们向着天国！

关于希腊文、拉丁文以及学院功课中的世俗部分，我不拟置评，只说一句：大抵收效甚微。神学与《圣经》的课业则是一场骗局。我们进学院，头一件本是为了学《圣经》。我们今后全部的人生都要以那本书为基础；我们的一生都要用来传讲它。我敢说，无论学生还是教授，再没有一本书比它被理解得更少的了。院长有一门讲座，年复一年地讲给一届又一届学生，讲的是《圣经》的真实性与灵感。那些讲稿全然不着边际；后来，等我明白了信仰的难处究竟何在，我才发现，这些被认为是对怀疑论者的凯旋驳斥的文章，不过是把木头剑。它们从未触及问题本身；倘若听众心里起了什么疑问，也没有人敢说出口，生怕一吐露便招来异端之嫌。

===Original===
I remember also some lectures on the proof of the existence of God and on the argument from design; all of which, when my mind was once awakened, were as irrelevant as the chattering of sparrows. When I did not even know who or what this God was, and could not bring my lips to use the word with any mental honesty, of what service was the “watch argument” to me? Very lightly did the President pass over all these initial difficulties of his religion. I see him now, a gentleman with lightish hair, with a most mellifluous voice and a most pastoral manner, reading his prim little tracts to us directed against the “shallow infidel” who seemed to deny conclusions so obvious that we were certain he could not be sincere, and those of us who had never seen an infidel might well be pardoned for supposing that he must always be wickedly blind.

About a dozen of these tracts settled the infidel and the whole mass of unbelief from the time of Celsus downwards. The President’s task was all the easier because he knew nothing of German literature; and, indeed, the word “German” was a term of reproach signifying something very awful, although nobody knew exactly what it was.

Systematic theology was the next science to which the President directed us. We used a sort of Calvinistic manual which began by setting forth that mankind was absolutely in God’s power. He was our maker, and we had no legal claim whatever to any consideration from Him. The author then mechanically built up the Calvinistic creed, step by step, like a house of cards. Systematic theology was the great business of our academical life. We had to read sermons to the President in class, and no sermon was considered complete and proper unless it unfolded what was called the scheme of redemption from beginning to end.

===Chinese===
我还记得几讲关于上帝存在的证明、关于目的论论证的课；待我的心智一旦觉醒，这些全都像麻雀的聒噪一样文不对题。我连这位上帝是谁、是什么都不知道，连凭一点内心的诚实念出这个名字都做不到，「钟表论证」于我又有何用？院长把他宗教的这一切入门难处，都轻轻一笔带过。我如今还看得见他：一位浅色头发的绅士，嗓音极甜润，态度极牧师气，给我们诵读他一册册古板的宣道小册，矛头指向那个「浅薄的不信者」；此人否定的结论似乎如此不言自明，以至于我们断定他不可能是真诚的；我们这些从未见过不信者的人，若猜想此人必定一贯地、恶意地瞎着眼，也大可得到原谅。

这一类小册子，大约一打上下，就把那不信者、连同自塞尔苏斯（Celsus）以来整堆整堆的不信仰统统打发掉了。院长的差事还格外省力，因为他对德国文学一无所知；而且事实上，「德国的」这个词在当时是个骂人的字眼，意味着某种非常可怕的东西——虽然谁也说不准那到底是什么。

院长引导我们攻读的下一门学问是系统神学。我们用一种加尔文主义的手册，开篇便申明：人类绝对处于上帝的权柄之下。祂是我们的造物主，我们在法理上无论如何都无权向祂要求任何体恤。作者随即一步一步、机械地把加尔文派信条搭起来，像搭一座纸牌屋。系统神学是我们学院生活的头等大事。我们要在班上把讲章读给院长听，而一篇讲章，若不把那所谓的救赎方案从头至尾铺陈一遍，便不算完整得体。

===Original===
So it came to pass that about the Bible, as I have already said, we were in darkness. It was a magazine of texts, and those portions of it which contributed nothing in the shape of texts, or formed no part of the scheme, were neglected. Worse still, not a word was ever spoken to us telling us in what manner to strengthen the reason, to subdue the senses, or in what way to deal with all the varied diseases of that soul of man which we were to set ourselves to save. All its failings, infinitely more complicated than those of the body, were grouped as “sin,” and for these there was one quack remedy. If the patient did not like the remedy, or got no good from it, the fault was his.

It is remarkable that the scheme was never of the slightest service to me in repressing one solitary evil inclination; at no point did it come into contact with me. At the time it seemed right and proper that I should learn it, and I had no doubt of its efficacy; but when the stress of temptation was upon me, it never occurred to me, nor when I became a minister did I find it sufficiently powerful to mend the most trifling fault. In after years, but not till I had strayed far away from the President and his creed, the Bible was really opened to me, and became to me, what it now is, the most precious of books.

There were several small chapels scattered in the villages near the college, and these chapels were “supplied,” as the phrase is, by the students. Those who were near the end of their course were also employed as substitutes for regular ministers when they were temporarily absent. Sometimes a senior was even sent up to London to take the place, on a sudden emergency, of a great London minister, and when he came back he was an object almost of adoration. The congregation, on the other hand, consisting in some part of country people spending a Sunday in town and anxious to hear a celebrated preacher, were not at all disposed to adore, when, instead of the great man, they saw “only a student.”

===Chinese===
于是，如我已说过的，对于《圣经》，我们身处黑暗之中。它是一仓库的经文；凡贡献不出经文、或入不了那套方案的部分，一概受到冷落。更糟的是，从来没有人对我们讲过一句话，教我们如何强固理性、如何制服感官、如何对付人灵魂的种种疾病——而这灵魂，正是我们立志要去拯救的。灵魂的一切毛病，比肉体的毛病复杂千万倍，却被统统归作一类，名曰「罪」；对付它们，只有一副万灵丹。病人若不爱这药，或服了无益，错处全在他自己。

说也奇怪，这套方案在压制哪怕一个邪僻念头上，也从未给过我丝毫用处；在任何一点上，它都不曾与我接触。当时学会它，看来是理所应当的，我对它的功效也深信不疑；可当试探的重压临到身上时，我从不曾想起它；等我做了牧师，也不见它有力到能修正最微小的过失。多年以后——那已是在我远远离开院长和他的信条之后——《圣经》才真正向我敞开，成了它如今之于我的样子：最珍贵的一本书。

学院附近的村子里散布着几座小教堂，照当时的说法，这些教堂由学生「供应」。临近毕业的学生还被雇来顶替临时缺席的正任牧师。有时甚至会有一个高年级学生被急急派往伦敦，去代一位伦敦名牧讲道；等他回来，几乎成了众人崇拜的对象。反过来，会众中有一部分是进城过星期日、一心想听名布道家讲道的乡人，当他们看到的不是那位大人物，而「不过是个学生」时，可一点儿也不想崇拜。

===Original===
By the time I was nineteen I took my turn in “supplying” the villages, and set forth with the utmost confidence what appeared to me to be the indubitable gospel. No shadow of a suspicion of its truth ever crossed my mind, and yet I had not spent an hour in comprehending, much less in answering, one objection to it. The objections, in fact, had never met me; they were over my horizon altogether. It is wonderful to think how I could take so much for granted; and not merely take it to myself and for myself, but proclaim it as a message to other people. It would be a mistake, however, to suppose that theological youths are the only class who are guilty of such presumption. Our gregarious instinct is so strong that it is the most difficult thing for us to be satisfied with suspended judgment. Men must join a party, and have a cry, and they generally take up their party and their cry from the most indifferent motives.

For my own part I cannot be enthusiastic about politics, except on rare occasions when the issue is a very narrow one. There is so much that requires profound examination, and it disgusts me to get upon a platform and dispute with ardent Radicals or Conservatives who know nothing about even the rudiments of history, political economy, or political philosophy, without which it is as absurd to have an opinion upon what are called politics as it would be to have an opinion upon an astronomical problem without having learned Euclid.

The more incapable we are of thorough investigations, the wider and deeper are the subjects upon which we busy ourselves, and still more strange, the more bigoted do we become in our conclusions about them; and yet it is not strange, for he who by painful processes has found yes and no alternate for so long that he is not sure which is final, is the last man in the world, if he for the present is resting in yes, to crucify another who can get no further than no. The bigot is he to whom no such painful processes have ever been permitted.

===Chinese===
到我十九岁，也轮到我「供应」那些村子了；我满怀极度的自信，宣讲着在我看来无可置疑的福音。对其真实性，我心中从未掠过一丝怀疑的影子；然而，为理解一条对它的反驳——更不必说回应了——我连一个钟头也不曾花过。那些反驳其实从未与我相遇；它们全然在我的地平线之外。想想我竟能把这么多东西视为理所当然——而且不只是自己领受、为自己领受，还把它当作信息传给他人——实在令人称奇。不过，若以为只有学神学的青年才犯这种狂妄之罪，那就错了。我们的合群本能如此强烈，要满足于悬置判断，对我们是最难不过的事。人总要加入一党，总要有一句口号，而他们择党择口号，通常出于最无关紧要的动机。

就我自己而论，我无法对政治热心，除非在罕见的场合——争端极窄、一目了然之时。有太多东西需要深刻的检视；一想到要登上讲台，同满腔热忱的激进派或保守派争辩，而他们对历史、政治经济学或政治哲学的ABC都一无所知，我就生厌。没有这些学问而对所谓政治持有意见，就好比没学过欧几里得而对天文问题发表意见一样荒唐。

我们越是无力做透彻的研究，所忙碌的题目便越宽越深；更奇怪的是，我们对这些题目所下的结论也越发武断。然而这并不奇怪：一个人若经过痛苦的过程，长年累月地发现「是」与「否」交替出现，久到自己都不确定哪个是最终的；那么，只要他此刻安歇在「是」里，他便是世上最不可能去把另一个只能走到「否」的人钉上十字架的人。偏执徒，则是从未被允许经历这种痛苦过程的人。

===Original===
The society amongst the students was very poor. Not a single friendship formed then has remained with me. They were mostly young men of no education, who had been taken from the counter, and their spiritual life was not very deep. In many of them it did not even exist, and their whole attention was absorbed upon their chances of getting wealthy congregations or of making desirable matches. It was a time in which the world outside was seething with the ferment which had been cast into it by Germany and by those in England whom Germany had influenced, but not a fragment of it had dropped within our walls. I cannot call to mind a single conversation upon any but the most trivial topics, nor did our talk ever turn even upon our religion, so far as it was a thing affecting the soul, but upon it as something subsidiary to chapels, “causes,” deacons, and the like.

The emptiness of some of my colleagues, and their worldliness, too, were almost incredible. There was one who was particularly silly. He was a blond youth with greyish eyes, a mouth not quite shut, and an eternal simper upon his face. He never had an idea in his head, and never read anything except the denominational newspapers and a few well-known aids to sermonising. He was a great man at all tea-meetings, anniversaries, and parties. He was facile in public speaking, and he dwelt much upon the joys of heaven and upon such topics as the possibility of our recognising one another there. I have known him describe for twenty minutes, in a kind of watery rhetoric, the passage of the soul to bliss through death, and its meeting in the next world with those who had gone before.

With all his weakness he was close and mean in money matters, and when he left college, the first thing he did was to marry a widow with a fortune. Before long he became one of the most popular of ministers in a town much visited by sick persons, with whom he was an especial favourite. I disliked him﻿—and specially disliked his unpleasant behaviour to women. If I had been a woman, I should have spurned him for his perpetual insult of inane compliments. He was always dawdling after “the sex,” which was one of his sweet phrases, and yet he was not passionate. Passion does not dawdle and compliment, nor is it nasty, as this fellow was. Passion may burn like a devouring flame; and in a few moments, like flame, may bring down a temple to dust and ashes, but it is earnest as flame, and essentially pure.

During the first two years at college my life was entirely external. My heart was altogether untouched by anything I heard, read, or did, although I myself supposed that I took an interest in them. But one day in my third year, a day I remember as well as Paul must have remembered afterwards the day on which he went to Damascus, I happened to find amongst a parcel of books a volume of poems in paper boards. It was called *Lyrical Ballads*, and I read first one and then the whole book. It conveyed to me no new doctrine, and yet the change it wrought in me could only be compared with that which is said to have been wrought on Paul himself by the Divine apparition.

===Chinese===
学生之间的交游十分贫乏。那时结下的一份友谊也没有留到今天。他们多半是没有受过教育的青年，是从柜台后面抽出来的，属灵的生命不深；在许多人身上，这生命甚至根本不存在，全部心思都放在谋一个有钱的教会、或攀一门好亲事的机会上。那正是外面的世界因德国、以及英国受德国影响之人所投入的酵而沸腾翻滚的时代；可这酵的一星半点也没有落进我们的院墙之内。我记不起有过一次超出最琐碎话题的谈话；我们的话题也从不涉及我们的宗教——就宗教作为关乎灵魂之事而言——只把它当作附属于教堂、「圣工」、执事等等的东西来谈。

我一些同窗的空虚，连同他们的俗气，几乎令人难以置信。有一个尤其蠢。他是个金发青年，灰眼睛，嘴总闭不严，脸上挂着一副永恒的痴笑。他脑袋里从来没有一个念头，除宗派报纸和几本人所共知的讲章范文之外什么都不读。凡茶会、周年庆典、联欢聚会，他都是个大人物。他擅长当众讲话，最爱大谈天国的喜乐，以及我们到那边能否彼此相认之类的题目。我听他用一种稀汤寡水的辞藻，足足花二十分钟描绘灵魂如何穿过死亡抵达至福，如何在来世与先行者相会。

尽管有这许多不济，他在钱财上却小气而吝啬；离开学院后，他做的头一件事便是娶一位带着财产的寡妇。不久，他就在一座病人常去疗养的城镇当上了最受欢迎的牧师之一，格外讨病人喜欢。我不喜欢他——尤其不喜欢他对女人那副叫人不舒服的做派。我若是个女人，定会把他一脚踢开，因为他那没完没了的空泛恭维是一种不休的侮辱。他总在「女性」屁股后面打转——这是他的甜言蜜语之一——然而他并不热烈。真正的情欲不这样打转、不这样恭维，也不像这家伙这样龌龊。情欲可以烧得像吞灭一切的火焰，顷刻间像火焰一样把一座神庙夷为尘土灰烬；但它如火焰般恳切，而且本质纯洁。

在学院的头两年，我的生活全然是外在的。我所听闻、阅读、做的一切，都丝毫没有触动我的心，尽管我自己还以为对它们有兴趣。可是第三年里的某一天——这一天我记得之清楚，一如保罗后来必定记得他往大马士革去的那一天——我在一包书里偶然翻到一册纸面装帧的诗集。它叫《抒情歌谣集》（*Lyrical Ballads*），我先读了一首，随即读完了全书。它没有传给我任何新教义，然而它在我身上造成的变化，唯有说是在保罗本人身上由属天的异象所造成的那种变化，才可比拟。

===Original===
Looking over the *Lyrical Ballads* again, as I have looked over it a dozen times since then, I can hardly see what it was which stirred me so powerfully, nor do I believe that it communicated much to me which could be put in words. But it excited a movement and a growth which went on till, by degrees, all the systems which enveloped me like a body gradually decayed from me and fell away into nothing. Of more importance, too, than the decay of systems was the birth of a habit of inner reference and a dislike to occupy myself with anything which did not in some way or other touch the soul, or was not the illustration or embodiment of some spiritual law.

There is, of course, a definite explanation to be given of one effect produced by the *Lyrical Ballads*. God is nowhere formally deposed, and Wordsworth would have been the last man to say that he had lost his faith in the God of his fathers. But his real God is not the God of the Church, but the God of the hills, the abstraction Nature, and to this my reverence was transferred. Instead of an object of worship which was altogether artificial, remote, never coming into genuine contact with me, I had now one which I thought to be real, one in which literally I could live and move and have my being, an actual fact present before my eyes. God was brought from that heaven of the books, and dwelt on the downs in the faraway distances, and in every cloud-shadow which wandered across the valley. Wordsworth unconsciously did for me what every religious reformer has done﻿—he recreated my Supreme Divinity; substituting a new and living spirit for the old deity, once alive, but gradually hardened into an idol.

What days were those of the next few years before increasing age had presented preciser problems and demanded preciser answers; before all joy was darkened by the shadow of oncoming death, and when life seemed infinite! Those were the days when through the whole long summer’s morning I wanted no companion but myself, provided only I was in the country, and when books were read with tears in the eyes. Those were the days when mere life, apart from anything which it brings, was exquisite.

In my own college I found no sympathy, but we were in the habit of meeting occasionally the students from other colleges, and amongst them I met with one or two, especially one who had undergone experiences similar to my own. The friendships formed with these young men have lasted till now, and have been the most permanent of all the relationships of my existence. I wish not to judge others, but the persons who to me have proved themselves most attractive, have been those who have passed through such a process as that through which I myself passed; those who have had in some form or other an enthusiastic stage in their history, when the story of Genesis and of the Gospels has been rewritten, when God has visibly walked in the garden, and the Son of God has drawn men away from their daily occupations into the divinest of dreams.

===Chinese===
后来我把《抒情歌谣集》重翻过——自那以后翻过不下十几次——却几乎看不出当初是什么东西如此强烈地撼动了我；我也不相信它传给我的东西有多少能诉诸言语。但它唤起了一场运动、一番生长，持续不已，直到包裹我周身如同一件躯壳的一切体系，渐渐从我身上朽坏、剥落，归于乌有。比体系的朽坏更要紧的，是一种内心参照的习惯的诞生，以及一种厌恶——厌恶把心思用在任何不以某种方式触及灵魂、或不为某条属灵法则作例证或化身的事物上。

《抒情歌谣集》所产生的诸般效果之一，当然可以给出一个明确的解释。书中没有一处正式废黜上帝；华兹华斯（Wordsworth）也断然是最后一个肯说自己丧失了列祖之上帝的信仰的人。但他真正的上帝不是教会的上帝，而是群山的上帝，那个抽象之物——自然；我的崇敬便转移到这上面来了。原先那敬拜的对象全然是人工的、遥远的、从不与我发生真实接触；如今我有了一个我信以为真的对象，一个我实实在在能在其中生活、动作、存有的对象，一件明明白白呈现在我眼前的实际。上帝从书本里那个天国被请了下来，住在远方遥远的丘陵上，住在每一片飘过山谷的云影里。华兹华斯不自觉地为我做了每一代宗教改革者所做的事——他重造了我的至高神；以一位新的、活着的灵，替换了那位旧日的神——祂曾一度活着，却渐渐硬化成了一尊偶像。

此后几年的时光是何等的日子啊——那时年岁渐长尚未提出更精确的问题、索要更精确的答案；死亡将临的阴影还没有罩暗一切欢乐，而生命仿佛是无穷无尽的！在那些日子里，只要身在乡间，整个漫长的夏日之晨，我除自己之外不需要任何同伴；在那些日子里，读书是含着泪读的。在那些日子里，生命本身——撇开它带来的一切——就是精美的。

在我自己的学院里，我找不到共鸣；但我们偶尔会与其他学院的学生相聚，在其中我遇到一两个——特别是一个——经历过与我相似的心路。与这些青年结下的友谊一直存留到今天，是我一生一切关系中最持久的。我不想评判旁人，但在我看来证明了自己最有魅力的人，正是那些经历过我所经历之过程的人：他们的生命史上都曾有过这样或那样一段狂热的阶段——《创世记》与福音书的故事重写过了，上帝分明在园中行走，上帝之子把人从日常事务中引开，引入那最属神的梦境。

===Original===
I have known men﻿—most interesting men with far greater powers than any which I have possessed, men who have never been trammelled by a false creed, who have devoted themselves to science and acquired a great reputation, who have somehow never laid hold upon me like the man I have just mentioned. He failed altogether as a minister, and went back to his shop, but the old glow of his youth burns, and will burn, forever. When I am with him our conversation naturally turns on matters which are of profoundest importance: with others it may be instructive, but I leave them unmoved, and I trace the difference distinctly to that visitation, for it was nothing else, which came to him in his youth.

The effect which was produced upon my preaching and daily conversation by this change was immediate. It became gradually impossible for me to talk about subjects which had not some genuine connection with me, or to desire to hear others talk about them. The artificial, the merely miraculous, the event which had no inner meaning, no matter how large externally it might be, I did not care for. A little Greek mythological story was of more importance to me than a war which filled the newspapers. What, then, could I do with my theological treatises?

It would be a mistake, however, to suppose that I immediately became formally heretical. Nearly every doctrine in the college creed had once had a natural origin in the necessities of human nature, and might therefore be so interpreted as to become a necessity again. To reach through to that original necessity; to explain the atonement as I believed it appeared to Paul, and the sinfulness of man as it appeared to the prophets, was my object. But it was precisely this reaching after a meaning which constituted heresy. The distinctive essence of our orthodoxy was not this or that dogma, but the acceptance of dogmas as communications from without, and not as born from within.

===Chinese===
