我也认识过一些人——极有趣的人，天资远胜于我，从未被虚假的信条束缚过，献身科学而享有盛名——可不知怎么，他们从不曾像方才提到的那个人那样抓住我。他做牧师全然失败，回自己的铺子去了；但他青年时代那团旧火仍在燃烧，并将永远燃烧。我同他在一起，话题自然转到那些最重大的事情上；同旁人谈话或许也有教益，但我离开他们时内心毫不动摇。我把这差别清清楚楚地追溯到降临于他青年时代的那次造访——那只能称之为造访——之上。

这一变化对我讲道和日常谈话产生的影响是立竿见影的。渐渐地，再让我谈论与我没有任何真实关联的题目，或听别人谈论这些题目，都变得不可能了。人工造作的、纯粹神迹式的、没有内在含义的事件——无论外表多大——我都不再在意。一小段希腊神话故事，在我看比一场占满报纸的战争更重要。那么，我拿我的那些神学论著还能怎么办呢？

然而，若以为我立即就在形式上变成了异端，那就错了。学院信条中的几乎每一条教义，都曾在人性的需要中有过自然的起源，因此都可以解释得重新成为一种必需。穿透到那原初的必需；照我相信它在保罗眼中的样子解释赎罪，照它在众先知眼中的样子解释人的罪性——这便是我的目标。然而恰恰是这种对意义的追索构成了异端。我们正统信仰的本质特征，不在这条或那条教义，而在于把教义当作从外部传来的训示来接受，而不当作从内部生发的东西。

===Original===
Heresy began, and in fact was altogether present, when I said to myself that a mere statement of the atonement as taught in class was impossible for me, and that I must go back to Paul and his century, place myself in his position, and connect the atonement through him with something which I felt. I thus continued to use all the terms which I had hitherto used; but an uneasy feeling began to develop itself about me in the minds of the professors, because I did not rest in the “simplicity” of the gospel. To me this meant its unintelligibility.

I remember, for example, discoursing about the death of Christ. There was not a single word which was ordinarily used in the pulpit which I did not use﻿—satisfaction for sin, penalty, redeeming blood, they were all there﻿—but I began by saying that in this world there was no redemption for man but by blood; furthermore, the innocent had everywhere and in all time to suffer for the guilty. It had been objected that it was contrary to our notion of an all-loving Being that He should demand such a sacrifice; but, contrary or not, in this world it was true, quite apart from Jesus, that virtue was martyred every day, unknown and unconsoled, in order that the wicked might somehow be saved. This was part of the scheme of the world, and we might dislike it or not, we could not get rid of it. The consequences of my sin, moreover, are rendered less terrible by virtues not my own. I am literally saved from penalties because another pays the penalty for me. The atonement, and what it accomplished for man, were therefore a sublime summing up as it were of what sublime men have to do for their race; an exemplification, rather than a contradiction, of Nature herself, as we know her in our own experience.

Now, all this was really intended as a defence of the atonement; but the President heard me that Sunday, and on the Monday he called me into his room. He said that my sermon was marked by considerable ability, but he should have been better satisfied if I had confined myself to setting forth as plainly as I could the “way of salvation” as revealed in Christ Jesus. What I had urged might perhaps have possessed some interest for cultivated people; in fact, he had himself urged pretty much the same thing many years ago, when he was a young man, in a sermon he had preached at the Union meeting; but I must recollect that in all probability my sphere of usefulness would lie amongst humble hearers, perhaps in an agricultural village or a small town, and that he did not think people of this sort would understand me if I talked over their heads as I had done the day before. What they wanted on a Sunday, after all the cares of the week, was not anything to perplex and disturb them; not anything which demanded any exercise of thought; but a repetition of the “old story of which, Mr. Rutherford, you know, we never ought to get weary; an exhibition of our exceeding sinfulness; of our safety in the Rock of Ages, and there only; of the joys of the saints and the sufferings of those who do not believe.”

===Chinese===
当我对自己说：只把课堂上教的赎罪论照本宣科一遍，对我是不可能的；我必须回到保罗和他的世纪，站到他的位置上，借着赎罪把它同我所感受的某种东西连结起来——异端便从此开始，而且事实上已然全然在场了。我于是继续使用着迄今用过的所有术语；但教授们心里对我的不安之感开始滋生，因为我不安歇在福音的「单纯」里。在我听来，「单纯」的意思就是不可解。

譬如我记得有一次讲基督之死。讲坛上惯用的字眼我一个不落——罪的补赎、刑罚、救赎的宝血，全都在——但我开头便说：在这个世上，人非流血便无救赎；而且，无辜者无时无地不在为有罪者受苦。有人反对说，一位全然是爱的存在者竟要求这样的牺牲，这与我们的观念相悖；可悖也罢不悖也罢，在这世上，即便撇开耶稣不谈，美德确实每天都在受难，无闻而无慰，好叫恶人无论如何能得救。这是世界方案的一部分；我们喜欢也罢不喜欢也罢，摆脱不掉。再者，我的罪的后果之所以变得不那么可怕，靠的是不属于我的美德。我实实在在免除了刑罚，因为有别人替我付了代价。因此，赎罪及其为人类成就的，可说是一次崇高的总括——崇高的人为他们的族类所必须做之事的总括；是自然本身的一个例证——而非驳斥——自然，一如我们在自身经验中所认识的那样。

然而这一切，本意实在是护卫赎罪论；可那个星期日院长听了我讲道，星期一便把我叫进他的房间。他说我的讲章颇见才具，但我若只满足于尽量明白地陈明那在基督耶稣里显明的「得救之道」，他就会更满意些。我所讲的，或许对有教养的人有些兴味；事实上，多年前他自己年轻时在联会聚会上讲的一篇道里，也讲过大同小样的东西；但我必须记着，我的效用之所，多半在卑微的听众当中，也许在某个农业村落或小镇，而他并不认为，我若像前一天那样高谈阔论越过他们的头顶，这等人能听得懂。他们劳碌了一个星期，星期日所要的，不是什么让他们困惑不安的东西，不是什么要动脑筋的东西，而是把那「古老的故事再讲一遍——拉瑟福德先生，您知道，我们对它永远不该厌倦；陈列一下我们深重的罪性；我们在万古磐石中、且唯在其中的安稳；圣徒的喜乐与不信者的苦楚」。

===Original===
His words fell on me like the hand of a corpse, and I went away much depressed. My sermon had excited me, and the man who of all men ought to have welcomed me, had not a word of warmth or encouragement for me, nothing but the coldest indifference, and even repulse.

It occurs to me here to offer an explanation of a failing of which I have been accused in later years, and that is secrecy and reserve. The real truth is, that nobody more than myself could desire self-revelation; but owing to peculiar tendencies in me, and peculiarity of education, I was always prone to say things in conversation which I found produced blank silence in the majority of those who listened to me, and immediate opportunity was taken by my hearers to turn to something trivial. Hence it came to pass that only when tempted by unmistakable sympathy could I be induced to express my real self on any topic of importance.

It is a curious instance of the difficulty of diagnosing (to use a doctor’s word) any spiritual disease, if disease this shyness may be called. People would ordinarily set it down to self-reliance, with no healthy need of intercourse. It was nothing of the kind. It was an excess of communicativeness, an eagerness to show what was most at my heart, and to ascertain what was at the heart of those to whom I talked, which made me incapable of mere fencing and trifling, and so often caused me to retreat into myself when I found absolute absense of response.

===Chinese===
他的话落在我身上，像一只死人的手；我走开去，意气大为消沉。我的讲章曾让我自己振奋，而这位在众人中最该欢迎我的人，对我竟无半句温暖或鼓励的话，只有最冷的淡漠，甚至排拒。

这里我想解释一下后来岁月里被人指责的一桩毛病：讳莫如深、缄默不露。实情是：再没有人比我更渴望自我剖白；但由于我性情的特殊倾向和所受教育的特殊，谈话中我总爱说一些话，而我又总发现，这些话在大多数听者那里换来的是一片空白的沉默，听众随即抓住机会把话头转到琐事上去。于是便有了这样的结果：唯有受到确凿无疑的共鸣引诱时，我才肯就任何要紧的题目吐露真实的自我。

这件事恰好说明，任何属灵的疾病（倘若这羞怯可称作疾病的话）要作出诊断（借用医生的字眼）是何等困难。人们通常把它归因于自恃自足，没有对交往的健康需要。其实全然不是这么回事。那是倾吐欲的过量，是一股急切，急于袒露我心中最要紧的东西，也急于探明同我交谈者心中最要紧的东西；正是这股急切，使我不屑于也做不到单纯的敷衍与调笑，也正因此，每当发现对方全无回应，我便常常退回到自己心里去。

===Original===
I am also reminded here of a dream which I had in these years of a perfect friendship. I always felt that, talk with whom I would, I left something unsaid which was precisely what I most wished to say. I wanted a friend who would sacrifice himself to me utterly, and to whom I might offer a similar sacrifice. I found companions for whom I cared, and who professed to care for me; but I was thirsting for deeper draughts of love than any which they had to offer; and I said to myself that if I were to die, not one of them would remember me for more than a week. This was not selfishness, for I longed to prove my devotion as well as to receive that of another. How this ideal haunted me! It made me restless and anxious at the sight of every new face, wondering whether at last I had found that for which I searched as if for the kingdom of heaven.

It is superfluous to say that a friend of the kind I wanted never appeared, and disappointment after disappointment at last produced in me a cynicism which repelled people from me, and brought upon me a good deal of suffering. I tried men by my standard, and if they did not come up to it I rejected them; thus I prodigally wasted a good deal of the affection which the world would have given me. Only when I got much older did I discern the duty of accepting life as God has made it, and thankfully receiving any scrap of love offered to me, however imperfect it might be.

I don’t know any mistake which I have made which has cost me more than this; but at the same time I must record that it was a mistake for which, considering everything, I cannot much blame myself. I hope it is amended now. Now when it is getting late I recognise a higher obligation, brought home to me by a closer study of the New Testament. Sympathy or no sympathy, a man’s love should no more fail towards his fellows than that love which spent itself on disciples who altogether misunderstood it, like the rain which falls on just and unjust alike.

===Chinese===
这里我还想起那些年里做过的一个梦：一段完美的友谊。我总觉得，无论同谁交谈，我总留下了一些没说出口的话，而那恰恰是我最想说的。我想要一位肯为我彻底舍己的朋友，我也可以为他作出同样的牺牲。我找到过一些同伴，我在乎他们，他们也自称在乎我；可我渴求的是比他们所能给的更深的爱的畅饮；我对自己说，倘若我死了，他们中没有一个人会记得我超过一个星期。这并非自私，因为我渴望证明自己的忠贞，一如渴望领受他人的忠贞。这理想何等地纠缠着我啊！它使我一见新的面孔便焦躁不安、悬着心，暗想我是否终于找到了自己寻寻觅觅的那样东西——寻它，如同寻天国一样。

不消说，我想要的那种朋友始终没有出现；失望复失望，终于在我身上生出一种愤世的冷峭，把人们从我身边推开，也给我自己带来不少苦楚。我用自己的尺度衡量别人，够不上的便拒之门外；就这样，我把世人本愿给我的许多情谊，挥霍殆尽。直到老得多的时候，我才看明一项本分：接受上帝所造的这个人生，并且怀着感谢，领受人家递来的任何一小片爱，无论它多么不完全。

我不知道自己还犯过什么比这代价更大的过失；但与此同时我必须记下：全面考虑起来，这个过失我不能太苛责自己。我希望它如今已经改了。如今，当日之将晚，我认出了一项更高的本分——由更细地研读《新约》而领会到的：无论有无共鸣，一个人对同胞的爱都不当止息，正如那爱虽然倾注在全然误解它的门徒身上，也不曾止息；像雨一样，落在义人身上，也落在不义的人身上。

## VIII Progress in Emancipation / 第八章 解脱的进展

===Original===
I was with my Unitarian congregation for about a twelvemonth. My life during that time, save so far as my intercourse with Mrs. Lane, and one other friend presently to be mentioned, was concerned, was as sunless and joyless as it had ever been. Imagine me living by myself, roaming about the fields, and absorbed mostly upon insoluble problems with which I never made any progress, and which tended to draw me away from what enjoyment of life there was which I might have had.

One day I was walking along under the south side of a hill, which was a great place for butterflies, and I saw a man, apparently about fifty years old, coming along with a butterfly-net. He did not see me, for he looked about for a convenient piece of turf, and presently sat down, taking out a sandwich-box, from which he produced his lunch. His occupation did not particularly attract me, but in those days, if I encountered a new person who was not repulsive, I was always as eager to make his acquaintance as if he perchance might solve a secret for me, the answer to which I burned to know. I have been disappointed so many times, and have found that nobody has much more to tell me, that my curiosity has somewhat abated, but even now, the news that anybody who has the reputation for intelligence has come near me, makes me restless to see him. I accordingly saluted the butterfly-catcher, who returned the salutation kindly, and we began to talk.

He told me that he had come seven miles that morning to that spot because he knew that it was haunted by one particular species of butterfly which he wished to get; and as it was a still, bright day, he hoped to find a specimen. He had been unsuccessful for some years. Presupposing that I knew all about his science, he began to discourse upon it with great freedom, and he ended by saying that he would be happy to show me his collection, which was one of the finest in the country.

===Chinese===
我在一位论派（Unitarian）的教会待了约莫一年。那段时日，除了与莱恩太太（Mrs. Lane）以及稍后将提到的另一位朋友的交往之外，我的生活一如既往地没有阳光、没有欢乐。试想我孤身一人过活，在田野间游荡，心思多半陷在那些无从解决的问题里——我在这些问题上从未有过寸进，它们反倒把我从本可以享受到的那一点点人生乐趣中越拖越远。

有一天，我正沿着一座小山的南麓走——那是个出产蝴蝶的好地方——看见一个约莫五十岁的男人提着捕蝶网走来。他没有看见我，只顾四下找一块合适的草皮，随即坐下，取出一个三明治盒，从里面拿出午饭。他的营生并没有特别吸引我；但在那些日子里，只要遇到一个不令人反感的新面孔，我总是急切地想结识他，仿佛他兴许能为我解开一个我焦灼地渴望知道答案的秘密似的。我失望过太多次，发现谁也没有多少东西可告诉我，好奇心已稍稍减退；可即便如今，一听说任何有聪明之名的人到了我附近，我仍会坐立不安，非见不可。于是我向这位捕蝶人打了个招呼，他和善地回了礼，我们便交谈起来。

他告诉我，那天早晨他赶了七英里路专程到那个地点，因为他知道那里出没着一种他想弄到手的蝴蝶；天气晴朗无风，他希望能觅得一只标本。一连几年他都没有成功。他默认我对他的这门学问了如指掌，便放开胆子畅谈起来，末了说，他乐意向我展示他的收藏——那是全国最精美的收藏之一。


===Original===
“But I forget,” said he, “as I always forget in such cases, perhaps you don’t care for butterflies.”

“I take much interest in them. I admire exceedingly the beauty of their colours.”

“Ah, yes, but you don’t care for them scientifically, or for collecting them.”

“No, not particularly. I cannot say I ever saw much pleasure in the mere classification of insects.”

===Chinese===
「可我忘了，」他说，「逢到这种情形我总是忘——您也许对蝴蝶并无兴趣吧。」

「我很有兴趣。它们色彩的美丽，我极为叹赏。」

「啊，是这样，可您不是从科学上关心它们，也不收藏它们。」

「不算特别有兴趣。光是把昆虫分类，我从未觉得其中有多大乐趣。」

===Original===
“Perhaps you are devoted to some other science?”

“No, I am not.”

“Well, I daresay it looks absurd for a man at my years to be running after a moth. I used to think it was absurd, but I am wiser now. However, I cannot stop to talk; I shall lose the sunshine. The first time you are anywhere near me, come and have a look. You will alter your opinion.”

===Chinese===
「那么您也许潜心于别的什么学问？」

「不，没有。」

「好吧，一个我这把年纪的人还追着蛾子跑，想必显得可笑。我从前也觉得可笑，如今我可明白多了。不过，我不能停下来说话了；再耽搁就要错过阳光了。日后您几时到了我附近，请来亲眼看一看。您会改变看法的。」


===Original===
Some weeks afterwards I happened to be in the neighbourhood of the butterfly-catcher’s house, and I called. He was at home, and welcomed me cordially. The first thing he did was to show me his little museum. It was really a wonderful exhibition, and as I saw the creatures in lines, and noted the amazing variations of the single type, I was filled with astonishment. Seeing the butterflies systematically arranged was a totally different thing from seeing a butterfly here and there, and gave rise to altogether new thoughts. My friend knew his subject from end to end, and I envied him his mastery of it. I had often craved the mastery of some one particular province, be it ever so minute. I half or a quarter knew a multitude of things, but no one thing thoroughly, and was never sure, just when I most wanted to be sure. We got into conversation, and I was urged to stay to dinner. I consented, and found that my friend’s household consisted of himself alone. After dinner, as we became a little more communicative, I asked him when and how he took to this pursuit.

“It will be twenty-six years ago next Christmas,” said he, “since I suffered a great calamity. You will forgive my saying anything about it, as I have no assurance that the wound which looks healed may not break out again. Suffice to say, that for some ten years or more my thoughts were almost entirely occupied with death and our future state. There is a strange fascination about these topics to many people, because they are topics which permit a great deal of dreaming, but very little thinking: in fact, true thinking, in the proper sense of the word, is impossible in dealing with them. There is no rigorous advance from one position to another, which is really all that makes thinking worth the name. Every man can imagine or say cloudy things about death and the future, and feel himself here, at least, on a level with the ablest brain which he knows.

“I went on gazing gloomily into dark emptiness, till all life became nothing for me. I did not care to live, because there was no assurance of existence beyond. By the strangest of processes, I neglected the world, because I had so short a time to be in it. It is with absolute horror now that I look back upon those days, when I lay as if alive in a coffin of lead. All passions and pursuits were nullified by the ever-abiding sense of mortality. For years this mood endured, and I was near being brought down to the very dust.

===Chinese===
几个星期后，我恰巧到了捕蝶人住处附近，便登门拜访。他在家，热忱地欢迎我。他做的头一件事便是领我看他的小博物馆。那真是一场奇观：看着这些生灵一排排陈列着，注意到单一类型那令人惊叹的种种变化，我心中充满诧异。系统地观看排列成行的蝴蝶，与东一只西一只地偶见蝴蝶，完全是两回事，会催生出全然崭新的思想。我的朋友对他的题目从头到尾无一不知，我羡慕他这份精通。我常常渴求精通某一门领域，哪怕再小也好。我对许许多多的东西都懂一半或四分之一，却没有一样贯通，而且偏偏在最想有把握的时候没有把握。我们攀谈起来，他留我吃饭。我应允了，发现我这位朋友的家眷只有他孤身一人。饭后，彼此的话多了些，我便问他何时、又如何迷上了这门营生。

「到下个圣诞节，就是二十六年前了，」他说，「我遭了一场大难。请原谅我提起它，因为我无从保证这看上去已经愈合的伤口不会重新溃裂。只消说一句：有十年之久，我的心思几乎全被死亡和我们死后的境况占据。这些题目对许多人有一种奇怪的魔力，因为它们容许人大做其梦，却几乎不容人思考：事实上，就这个词的正当意义而言，真正的思考在这些问题上是不可能的。从一步严谨地推进到另一步——这本是思考之所以配称思考的全部所在——在这里并不存在。谁都能就死亡和未来想入非非、说些云山雾罩的话，并且觉得自己至少在这里，同他所知道的那颗最能干的头脑平起平坐。

「我一路阴郁地凝视着黑暗的空虚，直到整个生命对我成了虚无。我不想活了，因为并无来世存在的保证。经过最不可思议的过程，我荒废了人世，只因我在世上的时日如此短促。如今回想那些日子，回想我像活人一样躺在铅棺里的光景，我只感到十足的恐怖。一切激情、一切追求，都被那无时不在的死之感化作虚空。这样的心境持续了多年，我几乎被拖到尘土里去。

===Original===
“At last, by the greatest piece of good fortune, I was obliged to go abroad. The change, and the obligation to occupy myself about many affairs, was an incalculable blessing to me. While travelling I was struck with the remarkable and tropical beauty of the insects, and especially of the butterflies. I captured a few, and brought them home. On showing them to a friend, learned in such matters, I discovered that they were rare, and I had a little cabinet made for them. I looked into the books, found what it was which I had got, and what I had not got.

“Next year it was my duty to go abroad again, and I went with some feeling akin to pleasure, for I wished to add to my store. I increased it considerably, and by the time I returned I had as fine a show as any private person might wish to possess. A good deal of my satisfaction, perhaps, was unaccountable, and no rational explanation can be given of it. But men should not be too curious in analysing and condemning any means which Nature devises to save them from themselves, whether it be coins, old books, curiosities, butterflies, or fossils. And yet my newly-acquired passion was not altogether inexplicable. I was the owner of something which other persons did not own, and in a little while, in my own limited domain, I was supreme. No man either can study any particular science thoroughly without transcending it; and it is an utter mistake to suppose that, because a student sticks to any one branch, he necessarily becomes contracted.

“However, I am not going to philosophise; I do not like it. All I can say is, that I shun all those metaphysical speculations of former years as I would a path which leads to madness. Other people may be able to occupy themselves with them and be happy; I cannot. I find quite enough in my butterflies to exercise my wonder, and yet, on the other hand, my study is not a mere vacant, profitless stare. When you saw me that morning, I was trying to obtain an example which I have long wanted to fill up a gap. I have looked for it for years, but have missed it. But I know it has been seen lately where we met, and I shall triumph at last.”

===Chinese===
「最后，托了天大的好运，我不得不出国一趟。环境一换，又有诸务缠身，这于我是无可估量的恩惠。旅途中，那些昆虫——尤其是蝴蝶——的热带之美令我惊叹。我捕了几只，带回家来。拿给一位精于此道的朋友一看，才发现它们竟是珍稀之物；我便定做了一只小橱柜盛放它们。我翻检书籍，弄清了自己已经到手的是什么，还没有到手的又是什么。

「第二年我又有公务出国，这一去竟带着几分近乎快乐的心情，因为我想给收藏添些新品。我着实添了不少，归国之时，我的收藏已如任何私人收藏者所能期望的那样可观。我这份满足，或许有一大半无从解释，也讲不出什么道理。可是，大自然用来拯救人脱离自我的任何法子——无论是钱币、古书、古玩、蝴蝶还是化石——人们都不必过分好奇地去剖析它、贬责它。话虽如此，我新染上的这份痴好也并非全然不可解。我拥有别人不曾拥有的东西，而且不久，在我自己这方有限的疆域之内，我便是至尊。再者，谁若把某一门科学钻研透彻，便不可能不超越它；以为治学者拘守一门便必然变得狭隘，那是大错特错。

「不过，我不打算谈哲理；我不喜欢谈哲理。我只消说一句：我躲避早年那一切形而上学的玄思，如同躲避一条通往疯狂的路。别人也许能与此类东西为伴并且快活；我不能。我在蝴蝶中觅得的，尽够激发我的惊奇；而另一方面，我的研究又不是徒然的、无益的呆望。您那天早晨见到我时，我正想猎取一个我盼望已久的标本，好填补一个空缺。我寻它已有多年，却屡屡错过。但我知道，近来在我们相遇的地方有人见过它，我终将得胜。」


===Original===
A good deal of all this was to me incomprehensible. It seemed mere solemn trifling compared with the investigation of those great questions with which I had been occupied, but I could not resist the contagion of my friend’s enthusiasm when he took me to his little library and identified his treasures with pride, pointing out at the same time those in which he was deficient. He was specially exultant over one minute creature which he had caught himself, which he had not as yet seen figured, and he proposed going to the British Museum almost on purpose to see if he could find it there.

When I got home I made inquiries into the history of my entomologist. I found that years ago he had married a delicate girl, of whom he was devotedly fond. She died in childbirth, leaving him completely broken. Her offspring, a boy, survived, but he was a cripple, and grew up deformed. As he neared manhood he developed a satyr-like lustfulness, which was almost uncontrollable, and made it difficult to keep him at home without constraint. He seemed to have no natural affection for his father, nor for anybody else, but was cunning with the base, beastly cunning of the ape. The father’s horror was infinite. This thing was his only child, and the child of the woman whom he worshipped. He was excluded from all intercourse with friends; for, as the boy could not be said to be mad, he could not be shut up. After years of inconceivable misery, however, lust did deepen into absolute lunacy, and the crooked, misshapen monster was carried off to an asylum, where he died, and the father well-nigh went there too.

Before I had been six months amongst the Unitarians, I found life even more intolerable with them than it had been with the Independents. The difference of a little less belief was nothing. The question of Unitarianism was altogether dead to me; and although there was a phase of the doctrine of God’s unity which would now and then give me an opportunity for a few words which I felt, it was not a phase for which my hearers in the least cared or which they understood.

===Chinese===
这一切，有许多我无法领会。比起我素来萦怀的那些大问题的探究，这似乎只是庄严其事的琐碎；可当他领我到他的小书房，自豪地一一鉴定他的珍藏，同时又指出他还缺哪些时，我抵挡不住他那份热忱的感染。有一只微小的生物是他亲手捕得、至今未见有人图绘过的，他对它尤其得意，几乎要专程去一趟大英博物馆，看看能否在那里找到它的图录。

回家后，我打听了这位昆虫学家的身世。原来多年以前，他娶了一位纤弱的姑娘，爱她爱得至深。她死于难产，留他彻底垮了。她留下的孩子——一个男孩——活了下来，却是个残疾，长大后又生了畸形。临近成年，他生出一种萨蒂尔般的好色兽欲，几乎无法管束，想把他无拘无束地留在家中极难。他似乎对父亲没有半点天性之情，对谁都没有，只怀着猿猴那种卑劣的、兽性的狡黠。父亲的惊骇无以复加。这个东西是他唯一的孩子，又是他所崇敬的女人的孩子。他也因此断绝了与朋友的一切往来；因为那孩子既算不得疯，便不能关起来。然而，经过多年难以想象的苦楚，那淫欲终于深化为十足的精神失常，这个歪扭变形的怪物被送进了疯人院，死在那里；父亲自己，也险些跟着进去了。

在一位论派中间还不到半年，我便发现，同他们在一起的生活，比同公理会在一起时更难忍受。少信那么一点的差别，算不得什么。一位论这个问题，于我已经全然死了；尽管上帝独一这条教义有一个方面，偶尔能给我机会讲几句我心里感觉到的话，可听众对这一方面既毫无兴趣，也毫不理解。

===Original===
Here, as amongst the Independents, there was the same lack of personal affection, or even of a capability of it﻿—excepting always Mrs. Lane﻿—and, in fact, it was more distressing amongst the Unitarians than amongst the orthodox. The desire for something like sympathy and love absolutely devoured me. I dwelt on all the instances in poetry and history in which one human being had been bound to another human being, and I reflected that my existence was of no earthly importance to anybody. I could not altogether lay the blame on myself. God knows that I would have stood against a wall and have been shot for any man or woman whom I loved, as cheerfully as I would have gone to bed, but nobody seemed to wish for such a love, or to know what to do with it.

Oh, the humiliations under which this weakness has bent me! Often and often I have thought that I have discovered somebody who could really comprehend the value of a passion which could tell everything and venture everything. I have overstepped all bounds of etiquette in obtruding myself on him, and have opened my heart even to shame. I have then found that it was all on my side. For every dozen times I went to his house, he came to mine once, and only when pressed: I have languished in sickness for a month without his finding it out; and if I were to drop into the grave, he would perhaps never give me another thought. If I had been born a hundred years earlier, I should have transferred this burning longing to the unseen God and have become a devotee. But I was a hundred years too late, and I felt that it was mere cheating of myself and a mockery to think about love for the only God whom I knew﻿—the forces which maintained the universe.

I am now getting old, and have altered in many things. The hunger and thirst of those years have abated, or rather, the fire has had ashes heaped on it, so that it is well-nigh extinguished. I have been repulsed into self-reliance and reserve, having learned wisdom by experience; but still I know that the desire has not died, as so many other desires have died, by the natural evolution of age. It has been forcibly suppressed, and that is all. If anybody who reads these words of mine should be offered by any young dreamer such a devotion as I once had to offer, and had to take back again refused so often, let him in the name of all that is sacred accept it. It is simply the most precious thing in existence. Had I found anybody who would have thought so, my life would have been redeemed into something which I have often imagined, but now shall never know.

===Chinese===
在这里，也同在公理会那里一样，缺少人与人的情爱，甚至连爱人的能力也缺少——莱恩太太始终是例外——而事实上，在一位论派中间，这比在正统派中间更令人痛苦。对几分共鸣与爱的渴求，简直把我吞噬了。我细细想来，诗里、史书里那些一个人与另一个人结下生死之谊的例子；而我反省自己：我的存在对任何人都没有半点实际的分量。这不能全怪我。上天知道，凡是我所爱的人，无论男女，我都乐意背墙而立、为他吃一颗枪子，而且会像上床睡觉一样心甘情愿；可似乎没有人要这样的爱，也没有人知道拿它怎么办。

唉，这桩弱点让我受了多少屈辱啊！一次又一次，我以为自己发现了一个真正能领会这种激情之价值的人——一种可以倾吐一切、也豁得出去一切的激情。我逾越一切礼数强自亲近他，敞开胸怀，甚至到了不知羞耻的地步。随后我便发现，一切都只是我单方面的事。我登他家门十二次，他才回访我一次，还是再三敦请之下才来；我缠绵病榻一个月，他也不曾发觉；倘若我此刻跌进坟墓，他或许再也不会想起我。我若早生一百年，就会把这团炽热的渴慕转移到那位看不见的上帝身上，成为一名苦修的虔徒。可惜我迟了一百年；而我感到，向我所识的唯一上帝——那维系着宇宙的种种力量——去谈爱，不过是自欺，是嘲弄。

如今我渐渐老了，许多方面都变了。那些年月的饥渴已经平息，或者说，那团火上已经堆了灰，几乎压熄了。我因屡遭推拒而学会了自立与缄默，从经验里学得了明智；但我依然知道，这份欲望并没有像别的许多欲望那样，随着年岁的自然演进死去。它只是被强行压住了，如此而已。倘若读到这些文字的人，有朝一日得到某位年轻梦想者献上的这样一份忠贞——像我当年所要献出的、又不得不一次次收回的那一份——请他以一切神圣之物的名义收下它。它实在是人世间最珍贵的东西。倘若我曾遇到一个这样想的人，我的生命就会被救赎成我常常想象、如今却永远无从知晓的那种样子。


===Original===
I determined to leave, but what to do I could not tell. I was fit for nothing, and yet I could not make up my mind to accept a life which was simply living. It must be a life, through which some benefit was conferred upon my fellow-creatures. This was mainly delusion. I had not then learned to correct this natural instinct to be of some service to mankind by the thought of the boundlessness of infinity and of Nature’s profuseness. I had not come to reflect that, taking into account her eternities, and absolute exhaustlessness, it was folly in me to fret and fume, and I therefore clung to the hope that I might employ myself in some way which, however feebly, would help mankind a little to the realisation of an ideal. But I was not the man for such a mission. I lacked altogether that concentration which binds up the scattered powers into one resistless energy, and I lacked faith. All I could do was to play the vagrant in literature, picking up here and there an idea which attracted me, and presenting it to my flock on the Sunday; the net result being next to nothing.

However, existence like that which I had been leading was intolerable, and change it I must. I accordingly resigned, and with ten pounds in my pocket, which was all that remained after paying my bills, I came to London, thinking that until I could settle what to do, I would try and teach in a school. I called on an agent somewhere near the Strand, and after a little negotiation, was engaged by a gentleman who kept a private establishment at Stoke Newington.

Thither I accordingly went one Monday afternoon in January, about two days before the term commenced. When I got there, I was shown into a long schoolroom, which had been built out from the main building. It was dark, save for one candle, and was warmed by a stove. The walls were partly covered with maps, and at one end of the room hung a diagram representing a globe, on which an immense amount of wasted ingenuity had been spent to produce the illusion of solidity. The master, I was told, was out, and in this room with one candle I remained till nine o’clock. At that time a servant brought me some bread and cheese on a small tray, with half-a-pint of beer. I asked for water, which was given me, and she then retired. The tray was set down on the master’s raised desk, and sitting there I ate my supper in silence, looking down upon the dimly-lighted forms, and forward into the almost absolute gloom.

===Chinese===
我决心离开，可离开之后做什么，我说不上来。我一无所长，却又不能甘心接受一种只是活着的活法。它必须是一种能给我的同胞带来些益处的人生。这多半是妄想。我那时还没有学会，用无限之无边无际、自然之丰沛不竭这类念头，来校正这种想为人类效些力的天性冲动。我还没有想到：考虑到自然的永恒与绝不枯竭，我在那里焦躁恳切，实属愚妄。于是我便抱着一线希望：也许我能以某种方式效力于世，哪怕再微弱，也能帮人类稍稍接近某个理想的实现。可我根本不是担此使命的人。我全然缺少那种把散乱的能力捆束成一股不可抗拒之劲道的专注，也缺少信仰。我所能做的，不过是在文学里做个流浪儿，东一处西一处拾起一个吸引我的念头，星期日捧给我的羊群；其净成就，几近于零。

然而，我过的这种日子是无法忍受的，非变不可。于是我辞了职，付清各账之后，口袋里揣着仅剩的十镑钱，来到了伦敦，心想在定下做什么之前，先试去学校教书。我去拜访了斯特兰德街（the Strand）附近某处的一个职业介绍人，略经磋商，便受雇于一位在斯托克纽温顿（Stoke Newington）开办私立学馆的先生。

于是，一月的某个星期一下午，在开学前大约两天，我便去了那里。到后，我被领进一间狭长的教室，是从主楼接建出来的。屋里黑着，只点一支蜡烛，靠一只火炉取暖。墙上挂着一半地图，教室一端悬着一幅画着地球的挂图，为造出球体的错觉，那上面不知耗费了多少枉抛的心思。据说校长出去了；我就在这间只有一支蜡烛的屋子里一直待到九点。届时，一个女仆用小托盘给我送来面包和干酪，还有半品脱啤酒。我要了水，她给了我，便退了出去。托盘搁在校长那高起的讲桌上；我坐在那里，默默吃着晚饭，俯视着幽暗灯光下的一排排课桌，望向那几乎全然的黑暗。

===Original===
At ten o’clock a man, who seemed as if he were the knife and boot-cleaner, came and said he would show me where I was to sleep. We passed through the schoolroom into a kind of court, where there was a ladder standing against a trapdoor. He told me that my bedroom was up there, and that when I got up I could leave the ladder down, or pull it up after me, just as I pleased.

I ascended and found a little chamber, duly furnished with a chest of drawers, bed, and washhand-stand. It was tolerably clean and decent; but who shall describe what I felt! I went to the window and looked out. There were scattered lights here and there, marking roads, but as they crossed one another, and now and then stopped where building had ceased, the effect they produced was that of bewilderment with no clue to it. Further off was the great light of London, like some unnatural dawn, or the illumination from a fire which could not itself be seen. I was overcome with the most dreadful sense of loneliness. I suppose it is the very essence of passion, using the word in its literal sense, that no account can be given of it by the reason.

Reflecting on what I suffered, then, I cannot find any solid ground for it, and yet there are not half-a-dozen days or nights of my life which remain with me like that one. I was beside myself with a kind of terror, which I cannot further explain. It is possible for another person to understand grief for the death of a friend, bodily suffering, or any emotion which has a distinct cause, but how shall he understand the worst of all calamities, the nameless dread, the efflux of all vitality, the ghostly, haunting horror which is so nearly akin to madness?

===Chinese===
十点钟，来了一个男人，看样子像是管磨刀擦靴的杂役，说要领我去睡觉的地方。我们穿过教室，进到一个小院似的地方，那里靠着一扇活板门立着一架梯子。他告诉我，我的卧室就在上面；还说早晨起床后，梯子是留在下面，还是随手收上来，悉听尊便。

我爬了上去，发现一间小屋，像样地备着五斗柜、床和洗脸架。屋里还算干净体面；可我当时的心情，谁能描摹！我走到窗前望出去。四下里散布着点点灯火，标出一条条道路；可它们互相交叉，又不时在建筑止步的地方戛然而止，给人的感觉是一片无从索解的迷乱。更远处，是伦敦那片巨大的光，像一场不自然的黎明，又像一场看不见火头的火灾映出的红光。我被一种最可怕的孤独之感压倒了。我想，这正是激情——取这个词的本义——的本质：理智对它讲不出任何缘由。

如今反省那晚所受的苦，我找不出任何实在的根由；可我一生中，像那一夜那样留存于心的日夜，不过寥寥数个。我因一种恐怖而失了魂，这恐怖我无法再多作解释。丧友之恸、肉体之痛、任何有明确起因的情绪，旁人都能理解；可是那万难中之最难者——无以名之的惧怖、生命力的整个外泄、那与疯狂只有一线之隔的鬼魅般缠人的恐怖——叫人如何理解？


===Original===
It is many years ago since that evening, but while I write I am at the window still, and the yellow flare of the city is still in my eyes. I remember the thought of all the happy homes which lay around me, in which dwelt men who had found a position, an occupation, and, above all things, affection. I know the causelessness of a good deal of all those panic fears and all that suffering, but I tremble to think how thin is the floor on which we stand which separates us from the bottomless abyss.

The next morning I went down into the schoolroom, and after I had been there for some little time, the proprietor of the school made his appearance. He was not a bad man, nor even unkind in his way, but he was utterly uninteresting, and as commonplace as might be expected after having for many years done nothing but fight a very uphill battle in boarding the sons of tradesfolk, and teaching them, at very moderate rates, the elements of Latin, and the various branches of learning which constitute what is called a commercial education. He said that he expected some of the boys back that day; that when they came, he should wish me to take my meals with them, but that meanwhile he would be glad if I would breakfast with him and his wife. This accordingly I did. What his wife was like I have almost entirely forgotten, and I only saw her once again. After breakfast he said I could go for a walk, and for a walk I went; wandering about the dreary, intermingled chaos of fields with damaged hedges, and new roads divided into building plots.

Meanwhile one or two of the boys had made their appearance, and I therefore had my dinner with them. After dinner, as there was nothing particular to do, I was again dismissed with them for a walk just as the light of the winter afternoon was fading. My companions were dejected, and so was I! The wind was southeasterly, cold, and raw, and the smoke came up from the region about the river and shrouded all the building plots in fog. I was now something more than depressed. It was absolutely impossible to endure such a state of things any longer, and I determined that, come what might, I would not stop. I considered whether I should leave without saying a word﻿—that is to say, whether I should escape, but I feared pursuit and some unknown legal proceedings.

===Chinese===
那晚距今已许多年了；可我一壁写作，一壁仿佛仍立在窗前，城市黄色的火光仍映在我眼里。我记得当时想到四围那一切安乐园满的家——住在里面的男人们找到了位置，找到了事业，而最要紧的是，找到了情爱。我知道那些恐慌与那些苦楚大多全无来由；可一想到我们脚下隔开无底深渊的地板何其单薄，我仍不禁战栗。

第二天早晨，我下楼进了教室；待了一小会儿，学馆的东家露面了。他不是坏人，就他那一套而言也说不上苛刻，只是索然无味得可以，也平庸得如所预料——多年来他别无所为，只是一场苦苦支撑的硬仗：招些商贾之家的儿子寄宿，以极低廉的束脩，教他们拉丁文入门和构成所谓商业教育的各门功课。他说当天盼着有几个学生返校；他们到了之后，他要我跟他们同桌用膳；眼下，他愿请我和他夫妇共进早餐。我照办了。他太太什么模样，我几乎全忘了，后来也只再见过她一面。早饭之后，他说我可以出去散散步；我便去了——在那一大片荒凉杂乱的混沌里游荡：篱笆破败的田野，和划成一块块建房地基的新路，交错在一起。

这时，一两个学生已经到了，我便同他们一起用午饭。饭后无事可做，正当冬天午后的天光渐暗时，我又被打发同他们一道散步。同伴们个个垂头丧气，我也是！风是东南风，又冷又湿；烟从河那一带升上来，把一块块建房地基都罩进雾里。我这时的心情已不止是沮丧。这样的日子是断然一刻也忍不下去了，我打定主意：无论怎样，决不留下。我盘算过要不要一句话不说就走——也就是要不要逃走；但我怕人家追赶，也怕什么说不清的法律纠葛。


===Original===
When I got home, therefore, I sought the principal, and informed him that I felt so unwell that I was afraid I must throw up my engagement at once. He naturally observed that this was a serious business for him; that my decision was very hasty﻿—what was the matter with me? I might get better; but he concluded, after my reiterated asseverations that I must go, with a permission to resign, only on one condition, that I should obtain an equally efficient substitute at the same salary. I was more agitated than ever. With my natural tendency to believe the worst, I had not the least expectation of finding anybody who would release me.

The next morning I departed on my errand. I knew a poor student who had been at college with me, and who had nothing to do, and to him I betook myself. I strove﻿—as even now I firmly believe﻿—not to make the situation seem any better than it was, and he consented to take it. I have no clear recollection of anything that happened till the following day, excepting that I remember with all the vividness of actual and present sensuous perception lugging my box down the ladder and sending for a cab. I was in a fever lest anything should arrest me, but the cab came, and I departed. When I had got fairly clear of the gates, I literally cried tears of joy﻿—the first and the last of my life. I am constrained now, however, to admit that my trouble was but a bubble blown of air, and I doubt whether I have done any good by dwelling upon it.

===Chinese===
于是回到学馆，我便去找东家，告诉他我身体非常不适，恐怕必须立刻辞掉这份聘约。他自然说这事于他非同小可；说我这决定太仓促了——我到底怎么了？兴许会好起来的；但在我再三申言非走不可之后，他总算准我辞职，只附一个条件：我须觅得一位同样称职的替手，薪金照旧。这一来我心绪更乱了。我天生爱往最坏处想，全然不指望能找到什么人替我脱身。

第二天早晨，我便出门办事去了。我认识一个当年同我一起上过学院的穷学生，正无事可做，我便投他去了。我尽量——如今我仍坚信这一点——没有把这差事的处境说得比实际更好，他答应了接手。此后直到第二天，发生了什么我已记不真切，只记得一件事，而且历历如在目前：我从梯子上把箱子一趟趟拖下来，叫了一辆马车。我焦急如焚，生怕有什么事把我拦下；马车还是来了，我便走了。等车出了大门、走远了，我当真喜极而泣——那是我生平第一遭，也是最后一遭的眼泪。不过，如今我不得不承认，我那场烦恼不过是一只吹出来的肥皂泡；絮絮叨叨讲这些，是否有益，我也怀疑。

## III Water Lane / 第三章 水巷

===Original===
I had now reached the end of my fourth year at college, and it was time for me to leave. I was sent down into the eastern counties to a congregation which had lost its minister, and was there “on probation” for a month. I was naturally a good speaker, and as the “cause” had got very low, the attendance at the chapel increased during the month I was there. The deacons thought they had a prospect of returning prosperity, and in the end I received a nearly unanimous invitation, which, after some hesitation, I accepted. One of the deacons, a Mr. Snale, was against me; he thought I was not “quite sound”; but he was overruled. We shall hear more of him presently. After a short holiday I entered on my new duties.

The town was one of those which are not uncommon in that part of the world. It had a population of about seven or eight thousand, and was a sort of condensation of the agricultural country round. There was one main street, consisting principally of very decent, respectable shops. Generally speaking, there were two shops of each trade; one which was patronised by the Church and Tories, and another by the Dissenters and Whigs. The inhabitants were divided into two distinct camps﻿—of the Church and Tory camp the other camp knew nothing. On the other hand, the knowledge which each member of the Dissenting camp had of every other member was most intimate.

The Dissenters were further split up into two or three different sects, but the main sect was that of the Independents. They, in fact, dominated every other. There was a small Baptist community, and the Wesleyans had a new redbrick chapel in the outskirts; but for some reason or other the Independents were really the Dissenters, and until the “cause” had dwindled, as before observed, all the Dissenters of any note were to be found on Sunday in their meetinghouse in Water Lane.

My predecessor had died in harness at the age of seventy-five. I never knew him, but from all I could hear he must have been a man of some power. As he got older, however, he became feeble; and after a course of three sermons on a Sunday for fifty years, what he had to say was so entirely anticipated by his congregation, that although they all maintained that the gospel, or, in other words, the doctrine of the fall, the atonement, and so forth, should continually be presented, and their minister also believed and acted implicitly upon the same theory, they fell away﻿—some to the Baptists, some to the neighbouring Independents about two miles off, and some to the Church, while a few “went nowhere.”

===Chinese===
这时我在学院的第四个年头已近尾声，该离校了。我被派到东部诸郡一个失去了牧师的教会，在那里「试用」（on probation）了一个月。我天生擅长讲道，又因「圣工」颇为不振，我在那里的那个月，教堂的出席人数见涨。执事们以为复兴有望，末了我几乎获全票邀请，迟疑了一阵之后，我接受了。执事中有一位斯内尔先生（Mr. Snale）反对我；他认为我「不够纯正」；但他被多数压了下去。此人后文还会提到。短假过后，我便走马上任了。

这座镇子是那一带并不少见的类型，人口约七八千，是四乡农业地区的一个浓缩。有一条大街，两旁多是相当体面、像样的铺子。大抵每一行当都有两家：一家由国教会与托利党人光顾，另一家归不奉国教派与辉格党人光顾。居民分成壁垒分明的两个营垒——国教与托利这一营，另一营对之一无所知。反过来，不奉国教营垒里每个人对其他一切成员的底细，却是了如指掌。

不奉国教派内部又分成两三个宗派，但主流是公理会。事实上，它凌驾于其他各派之上。浸礼会有一小群信徒，卫斯理宗在镇郊有一座新的红砖教堂；但不知怎的，公理会才是此地不奉国教派的正宗；而且，如前所述，在「圣工」衰微之前，每逢星期日，所有稍有头脸的不奉国教派人士都会出现在水巷（Water Lane）他们的会堂里。

我的前任在职去世，享年七十五。我从未见过他，但据我所闻，他想必颇有几分才具。然而年事渐高，人也衰弱了；每星期日三篇讲道讲了五十年，他要说的东西，会众早已料得一清二楚。于是，尽管他们都坚称福音——也就是堕落、赎罪等等那套教义——应当不断宣讲，他们的牧师也信奉并一律照此行事，他们还是渐渐散去了——有的去了浸礼会，有的去了两英里外邻镇的公理会，有的投了国教会，还有几个「哪儿也不去」。

===Original===
When I came I found that the deacons still remained true. They were the skeleton; but the flesh was so woefully emaciated, that on my first Sunday there were not above fifty persons in a building which would hold seven hundred. These deacons were four in number. One was an old farmer who lived in a village three miles distant. Ever since he was a boy he had driven over to Water Lane on Sunday. He and his family brought their dinner with them, and ate it in the vestry; but they never stopped till the evening, because of the difficulty of getting home on dark nights, and because they all went to bed in wintertime at eight o’clock.

Morning and afternoon Mr. Catfield﻿—for that was his name﻿—gave out the hymns. He was a plain, honest man, very kind, very ignorant, never reading any book except the Bible, and barely a newspaper save *Bell’s Weekly Messenger*. Even about the Bible he knew little or nothing beyond a few favourite chapters; and I am bound to say that, so far as my experience goes, the character so frequently drawn in romances of intense Bible students in Dissenting congregations is very rare. At the same time Mr. Catfield believed himself to be very orthodox, and in his way was very pious. I could never call him a hypocrite. He was as sincere as he could be, and yet no religious expression of his was ever so sincere as the most ordinary expression of the most trifling pleasure or pain.

The second deacon, Mr. Weeley, was, as he described himself, a builder and undertaker; more properly an undertaker and carpenter. He was a thin, tall man, with a tenor voice, and he set the tunes. He was entirely without energy of any kind, and always seemed oppressed by a world which was too much for him. He had depended a good deal for custom upon his chapel connection; and when the attendance at the chapel fell off, his trade fell off likewise, so that he had to compound with his creditors. He was a mere shadow, a man of whom nothing could be said either good or evil.

The third deacon was Mr. Snale, the draper. When I first knew him he was about thirty-five. He was slim, small, and small-faced, closely shaven, excepting a pair of little curly whiskers, and he was extremely neat. He had a little voice too, rather squeaky, and the marked peculiarity that he hardly ever said anything, no matter how disagreeable it might be, without stretching as if in a smile his thin little lips. He kept the principal draper’s shop in the town, and even Church people spent their money with him, because he was so very genteel compared with the other draper, who was a great red man, and hung things outside his window. Mr. Snale was married, had children, and was strictly proper. But his way of talking to women and about them was more odious than the way of a debauchee. He invariably called them “the ladies,” or more exactly, “the leedies”; and he hardly ever spoke to a “leedy” without a smirk and some faint attempt at a joke.


===Chinese===
我到任时，发现执事们依然坚守。他们是骨架；只是皮肉瘦得可怜，以致我头一个星期日讲道时，一座能容七百人的建筑里不足五十人。执事共四位。一位是住在三英里外村子里的老农。从少年时代起，他每星期日就赶车来水巷。他和家人自带午饭，在法衣室里吃；但他们从不在中途回家，一直待到晚上——因为黑夜里赶路回家不易，也因为入冬后他们全家八点就上床睡觉。

上午和下午的圣诗都由卡特菲尔德先生（Mr. Catfield）——这是他的名字——宣报。他是个质朴诚实的人，心地很善良，学问极浅陋，除《圣经》外从不读别的书，报纸也几乎只看一份《贝尔每周信使报》（*Bell’s Weekly Messenger*）。就连《圣经》，他除几章心爱的经文之外也所知无几；我必须说，就我的阅历而言，小说里常常描写的那种不奉国教教会中埋头苦研《圣经》的人物，实属罕见。与此同时，卡特菲尔德先生自以为十分正统，也自有他的一片虔诚。我绝不能说他伪善。他尽其所能地真诚，然而他的一切宗教言辞，从没有一句比得上人随口表达最微不足道的欢愉或疼痛时那般真诚。

第二位执事威利先生（Mr. Weeley），照他自己的说法，是个营造商兼殡仪商；更准确地说，是殡仪商兼木匠。他是个瘦高个子，有一副男高音嗓子，领唱定调的是他。他身上没有一丝一毫的活力，总像被一个他应付不来的世界压得喘不过气。他的生意素来多赖教堂里的人情；教堂的出席人数一落，他的买卖随之冷落，以致不得不与债权人订立减偿的和解。他不过是一道影子，一个说不出好坏、无可评说的人。

第三位执事就是布商斯内尔先生。我初识他时，他三十五岁上下。他身量瘦小，脸也小，胡子剃得精光，只留一对卷曲的小连鬓胡，浑身上下整洁极了。他嗓音也细，带着点吱吱声；最显著的特点是，无论说什么话——哪怕再不顺耳——他那两片薄薄的小嘴唇也总要咧一咧，仿佛在笑。他开着镇上最大的布店，连国教会的人也上他那儿花钱，因为比起另一位布商——一个红光满面的大块头，爱把货色挂出橱窗之外——他实在文雅得多。斯内尔先生已婚，有孩子，行事规行矩步、无可挑剔。可是他同女人讲话、谈论女人的那副做派，比浪荡子还叫人恶心。他一概称她们为「淑女们」，或者更准确地说，「淑——女——们」（leedies）；他同哪位「淑女」说话，几乎无不挂着假笑，还要勉强来上一句打趣。


===Original===
One of the customs of the chapel was what were called Dorcas meetings. Once a month the wives and daughters drank tea with each other; the evening being ostensibly devoted to making clothes for the poor. The husband of the lady who gave the entertainment for the month had to wait upon the company, and the minister was expected to read to them while they worked.

It was my lot to be Mr. Snale’s guest two or three times when Mrs. Snale was the Dorcas hostess. We met in the drawing-room, which was over the shop, and looked out into the town marketplace. There was a round table in the middle of the room, at which Mrs. Snale sat and made the tea. Abundance of hot buttered toast and muffins were provided, which Mr. Snale and a maid handed round to the party.

Four pictures decorated the walls. One hung over the mantelpiece. It was a portrait in oils of Mr. Snale, and opposite to it, on the other side, was a portrait of Mrs. Snale. Both were daubs, but curiously faithful in depicting what was most offensive in the character of both the originals, Mr. Snale’s simper being preserved; together with the peculiarly hard, heavy sensuality of the eye in Mrs. Snale, who was large and full-faced, correct like Mr. Snale, a member of the church, a woman whom I never saw moved to any generosity, and cruel not with the ferocity of the tiger, but with the dull insensibility of a cartwheel, which will roll over a man’s neck as easily as over a flint. The third picture represented the descent of the Holy Ghost; a number of persons sitting in a chamber, and each one with the flame of a candle on his head. The fourth represented the last day. The Son of God was in a chair surrounded by clouds, and beside Him was a flying figure blowing a long mail-coach horn. The dead were coming up out of their graves; some were half out of the earth, others three-parts out﻿—the whole of the bottom part of the picture being filled with bodies emerging from the ground, a few looking happy, but most of them very wretched; all of them being naked.

The first time I went to Mrs. Snale’s Dorcas gathering Mr. Snale was reader, on the ground that I was a novice; and I was very glad to resign the task to him. As the business in hand was weekday and secular, it was not considered necessary that the selected subjects should be religious; but as it was distinctly connected with the chapel, it was also considered that they should have a religious flavour. Consequently the Bible was excluded, and so were books on topics altogether worldly. Dorcas meetings were generally, therefore, shut up to the denominational journal and to magazines. Towards the end of the evening Mr. Snale read the births, deaths, and marriages in this journal. It would not have been thought right to read them from any other newspaper, but it was agreed, with a fineness of tact which was very remarkable, that it was quite right to read them in one which was “serious.” During the whole time that the reading was going on conversation was not arrested, but was conducted in a kind of half whisper; and this was another reason why I exceedingly disliked to read, for I could never endure to speak if people did not listen.

===Chinese===
教堂的惯例之一，是所谓「多加会」（Dorcas meetings）。每月一次，太太小姐们聚在一处喝茶；名义上，这个晚上是专为穷人缝制衣裳的。当月做东的那位女士的丈夫须侍候众人，牧师则要在众人做活时为她们朗读。

轮到斯内尔太太做多加会东道的那两三次，我恰好都在斯内尔先生府上做客。聚会在店铺楼上的客厅里举行，窗外正对着镇集广场。客厅当中摆一张圆桌，斯内尔太太坐在桌旁沏茶。热黄油吐司和松饼备得足足的，由斯内尔先生和一名女仆分送给众人。

墙上挂着四幅画装点门面。一幅悬在壁炉架上方，是斯内尔先生的油画肖像；与它相对的另一侧墙上，挂着斯内尔太太的肖像。两幅都是拙劣的涂鸦，却古怪地忠实，把两位真人性情中最惹人厌恶之处都画了出来：斯内尔先生那副痴痴的假笑得以保全；斯内尔太太眼中那种格外冷硬滞重的肉欲也分毫未失。斯内尔太太生得高大，一张宽脸，同斯内尔先生一样循规蹈矩，是教会的教友；这个女人，我从未见她动过一丝慷慨之心；她的残忍不是猛虎式的凶残，而是车轮式的麻木——轧过人的脖颈，同轧过一块燧石一样无动于衷。第三幅画画的是圣灵降临：一间屋子里坐着许多人，每人头顶一簇烛火。第四幅画的是末日：上帝之子端坐云端环绕的宝座上，身旁一个飞着的人物吹着一支长长的邮车号角。死者正从坟墓里上来：有的半身出土，有的已出来四分之三——画面整个下半幅挤满了从地里钻出来的身体，少数几个神情快活，大多数凄惨万状；而且个个赤身露体。

我头一回去斯内尔太太的多加会，斯内尔先生以我是新人为由，亲自担任朗读，我也乐得把差事让给他。因为手头的活计是平日的世俗之事，选的篇目倒不必是宗教的；但因为聚会明明与教堂相关，又认定篇目须带点宗教气味。于是《圣经》被排除在外，纯属世俗题目的书也同样被排除。多加会于是大抵只剩本宗派的报和几本杂志可读。晚会将尽时，斯内尔先生便读那报上的出生、死亡与婚讯。若换作任何别的报纸，读这些便被认为不妥；但众人一致认定——那分体察入微的分寸感实在令人叹服——读一份「严肃的」报上的，则完全妥当。朗读进行的全部时间里，谈话并不停止，只是压成一种半低语；这也是我极不喜欢朗读的另一原因，因为若无人听，我是断然开不了口的。

===Original===
At half-past eight the work was put away, and Mrs. Snale went to the piano and played a hymn tune, the minister having first of all selected the hymn. Singing over, he offered a short prayer, and the company separated. Supper was not served, as it was found to be too great an expense. The husbands of the ladies generally came to escort them home, but did not come upstairs. Some of the gentlemen waited below in the dining-room, but most of them preferred the shop, for, although it was shut, the gas was burning to enable the assistants to put away the goods which had been got out during the day.

When it first became my turn to read I proposed the *Vicar of Wakefield*; but although no objection was raised at the time, Mr. Snale took an opportunity of telling me, after I had got through a chapter or two, that he thought it would be better if it were discontinued. “Because, you know, Mr. Rutherford,” he said, with his smirk, “the company is mixed; there are young leedies present, and perhaps, Mr. Rutherford, a book with a more requisite tone might be more suitable on such an occasion.” What he meant I did not know, and how to find a book with a more requisite tone I did not know.

However, the next time, in my folly, I tried a selection from George Fox’s *Journal*. Mr. Snale objected to this too. It was “hardly of a character adapted for social intercourse,” he thought; and furthermore, “although Mr. Fox might be a very good man, and was a converted character, yet he did not, you know, Mr. Rutherford, belong to us.” So I was reduced to that class of literature which of all others I most abominated, and which always seemed to me the most profane﻿—religious and sectarian gossip, religious novels designed to make religion attractive, and other slip-slop of this kind. I could not endure it, and was frequently unwell on Dorcas evenings.

The rest of the small congregation was of no particular note. As I have said before, it had greatly fallen away, and all who remained clung to the chapel rather by force of habit than from any other reason. The only exception was an old maiden lady and her sister, who lived in a little cottage about a mile out of the town. They were pious in the purest sense of the word, suffering much from ill-health, but perfectly resigned, and with a kind of tempered cheerfulness always apparent on their faces, like the cheerfulness of a white sky with a sun veiled by light and lofty clouds. They were the daughters of a carriage-builder, who had left them a small annuity.


===Chinese===
八点半，活计收拾起来，斯内尔太太走到钢琴前弹起一首圣诗的调子——圣诗是由牧师先选定的。唱罢，牧师做一段简短祷告，众人便散了。晚饭是不供应的，因为大家发现那花费太大。女士们的丈夫多半来接她们回家，却不上楼来。有几位先生在楼下饭厅里等候，大多数却宁愿待在店铺里——铺子虽已打烊，煤气灯还亮着，好让伙计们把白天摆出来的货色收好。

头一回轮到我朗读时，我提议读《威克菲尔德的牧师》（*Vicar of Wakefield*）；当时虽无人反对，可等我念完一两章，斯内尔先生便寻了个机会告诉我，他想还是不读为好。「因为，您知道，拉瑟福德先生（Mr. Rutherford），」他带着假笑说，「在座的人杂；有年轻的淑——女——们在场，或许，拉瑟福德先生，换一本调门更端正些的书，在这种场合更合适些。」他是什么意思，我不明白；上哪儿去找一本调门更端正的书，我也不明白。

然而下一次，我一时糊涂，又试了乔治·福克斯（George Fox）《日记》（*Journal*）的选段。斯内尔先生对此也反对。照他想来，它「简直不适合社交场合」；而且，「福克斯先生虽说也许是个很好的人，是个皈依过的人，可他毕竟，您知道，拉瑟福德先生，不属于我们这一派」。于是我沦落到那一类文字上——那是我最最厌恶的一类，在我看也向来最为亵渎：宗教的与宗派的闲话，专为把宗教装点得可爱而写的宗教小说，以及诸如此类的其他浅薄货色。我实在受不了，多加会的晚上常常闹起病来。

小小会众的其余部分则无可称述。如前所说，人数早已大减，留下来的人都多半出于积习而依附着教堂，别无他故。唯一的例外是一位老小姐和她的妹妹，住在镇外约一英里的一座小屋里。她们的虔诚是这个词最纯粹意义上的虔诚：长年为病痛所苦，却毫无怨言，脸上始终带着一种经过调和的开朗，恰似白色的天空下，太阳蒙着轻云高翳时的那份明朗。她们是一位马车制造者的女儿，父亲给她们留下一笔小小的年金。


===Original===
Their house was one of the sweetest which I ever entered. The moment I found myself inside it, I became conscious of perfect repose. Everything was at rest; books, pictures, furniture, all breathed the same peace. Nothing in the house was new, but everything had been preserved with such care that nothing looked old. Yet the owners were not what is called old-maidish; that is to say, they were not superstitious worshippers of order and neatness.

I remember Mrs. Snale’s children coming in one afternoon when I was there. They were rough and ill-mannered, and left traces of dirty footmarks all over the carpet, which the two ladies noticed at once. But it made no difference to the treatment of the children, who had some cake and currant wine given to them, and were sent away rejoicing. Directly they had gone, the elder of my friends asked me if I would excuse her; she would gather up the dirt before it was trodden about. So she brought a dustpan and brush (the little servant was out) and patiently swept the floor. That was the way with them. Did any mischief befall them or those whom they knew, without blaming anybody, they immediately and noiselessly set about repairing it with that silent promptitude of nature which rebels not against a wound, but the very next instant begins her work of protection and recovery.

The Misses Arbour (for that was their name) mixed but little in the society of the town. They explained to me that their health would not permit it. They read books﻿—a few﻿—but they were not books about which I knew very much, and they belonged altogether to an age preceding mine. Of the names which had moved me, and of all the thoughts stirring in the time, they had heard nothing. They greatly admired Cowper, a poet who then did not much attract me.

The country near me was rather level, but towards the west it rose into soft swelling hills, between which were pleasant lanes. At about ten miles distant eastward was the sea. A small river ran across the High Street under a stone bridge; for about two miles below us it was locked up for the sake of the mills, but at the end of the two miles it became tidal and flowed between deep and muddy banks through marshes to the ocean. Almost all my walks were by the riverbank down to these marshes, and as far on as possible till the open water was visible. Not that I did not like inland scenery: nobody could like it more, but the sea was a corrective to the littleness all round me. With the ships on it sailing to the other end of the earth it seemed to connect me with the great world outside the parochialism of the society in which I lived.

===Chinese===
她们的屋子是我进过的最怡人的屋子。一踏进门，我便觉出一种完完全全的安宁。样样东西都在歇息；书籍、图画、家具，都吐纳着同一份静谧。屋里没有一样东西是新的，却都保养得如此经心，以至于没有一样显出旧来。然而主人并非所谓老处女式的做派；也就是说，她们并不是秩序与整洁的迷信崇拜者。

记得有一天下午我在那里，斯内尔家的孩子们进来了。他们粗野无礼，把满是泥的脚印踩得地毯上到处都是，两位小姐立刻便注意到了。但这丝毫不影响她们款待孩子：孩子们得了蛋糕和黑加仑子酒，欢欢喜喜地被打发走了。他们前脚一走，我的朋友中年长的那位便问我能否容她失陪一下；她要在泥污被踩得四处都是之前收拾干净。于是她取来簸箕和扫帚（小女仆出去了），耐心地把地板扫净。她们就是这样的。无论什么祸事落在她们自己或相识的人头上，她们从不责备任何人，只立即无声地动手补救，带着大自然那种不抗辩伤口、下一瞬便开始防护与愈合之工的沉默的敏捷。

阿伯姐妹（Misses Arbour）——这是她们的姓氏——很少与镇上的社交界往来。她们向我解释说，健康不允许。她们也读书——读几本——但那些书我知之甚少，而且全然属于早于我的一个时代。那些曾经感动过我的名字，那个时代一切涌动的思想，她们一无所闻。她们极推崇考珀（Cowper），这位诗人当时并不怎么吸引我。

我四近的乡野颇为平坦，向西却隆起一座座绵软的丘陵，丘间夹着宜人的小径。朝东约十英里便是海。一条小河在石桥下穿过大街；往下约两英里，河身因水磨之故设了闸；两英里尽处，河受潮汐，在又深又泥泞的两岸之间穿过沼泽注入大海。我的散步路线几乎全是沿河岸走到那些沼泽，再尽量往前，直到望见开阔的水面。倒不是我不喜欢内陆风光：没有人会比我更喜欢，只是海是对我四周狭小天地的一种矫正。海上的船扬帆驶向地球的另一端，它仿佛把我同我那个教区气十足的社会之外的广大世界连结了起来。

===Original===
Such was the town of C﻿—﻿—, and such the company amidst which I found myself. After my probation it was arranged that I should begin my new duties at once, and accordingly I took lodgings﻿—two rooms over the shop of a tailor who acted as chapel-keeper, pew-opener, and sexton. There was a small endowment on the chapel of fifty pounds a year, and the rest of my income was derived from the pew-rents, which at the time I took charge did not exceed another seventy.

The first Sunday on which I preached after being accepted was a dull day in November, but there was no dullness in me. The congregation had increased a good deal during the past four weeks, and I was stimulated by the prospect of the new life before me. It seemed to be a fit opportunity to say something generally about Christianity and its special peculiarities. I began by pointing out that each philosophy and religion which had arisen in the world was the answer to a question earnestly asked at the time; it was a remedy proposed to meet some extreme pressure. Religions and philosophies were not created by idle people who sat down and said, “Let us build up a system of beliefs upon the universe; what shall we say about immortality, about sin?” and so on. Unless there had been antecedent necessity there could have been no religion; and no problem of life or death could be solved except under the weight of that necessity. The stoical morality arose out of the condition of Rome when the scholar and the pious man could do nothing but simply strengthen his knees and back to bear an inevitable burden. He was forced to find some counterpoise for the misery of poverty and persecution, and he found it in the denial of their power to touch him. So with Christianity.

Jesus was a poor solitary thinker, confronted by two enormous and overpowering organisations﻿—the Jewish hierarchy and the Roman State. He taught the doctrine of the kingdom of heaven; He trained Himself to have faith in the absolute monarchy of the soul, the absolute monarchy of His own; He tells us that each man should learn to find peace in his own thoughts, his own visions. It is a most difficult thing to do; most difficult to believe that my highest happiness consists in my perception of whatever is beautiful. If I by myself watch the sun rise, or the stars come out in the evening, or feel the love of man or woman﻿—I ought to say to myself, “There is nothing beyond this.” But people will not rest there; they are not content, and they are forever chasing a shadow which flies before them, a something external which never brings what it promises.

I said that Christianity was essentially the religion of the unknown and of the lonely; of those who are not a success. It was the religion of the man who goes through life thinking much, but who makes few friends and sees nothing come of his thoughts. I said a good deal more upon the same theme which I have forgotten.


===Chinese===
C——镇便是如此，我置身其间的人们便是如此。试用期结束后，安排我即刻上任，我便落了脚——住在一位裁缝店铺楼上的两个房间里；这位裁缝兼任教堂看门人、引座员和司事。教堂有一笔小小的捐助基金，每年五十镑，我收入的其余部分来自座位租金，我接手时，这部分还超不过七十镑。

蒙接纳后我讲道的头一个星期日，是十一月里一个阴沉的日子，我心里却毫无阴沉。过去四个星期会众增加了很多，眼前新生活的前景令我振奋。这似乎是个合适的时机，可以就基督教及其特殊之处总论一番。我开宗明义地指出：世上兴起的每一种哲学与宗教，都是对当时一个恳切提出的问题的回答；是为应对某种极端重压而提出的疗方。宗教与哲学并非闲人坐着想出来的——「让我们来建一套关于宇宙的信仰体系吧；关于不朽、关于罪，我们该怎么说？」云云。若无先行的迫需，便不会有宗教；生死大问题，也唯有在那迫需的重压之下方能解决。斯多噶派的道德产生于罗马的时势：那时学者与虔诚者无事可为，只能锻炼自己的膝盖与脊背，去扛起一副无可逃卸的重担。他不得不为贫穷与迫害的苦痛找一副平衡的砝码，他找到了，那就是否认它们有触及自己的力量。基督教亦然。

耶稣（Jesus）是一个贫穷而孤独的思想者，面前对峙着两个庞大得令人生畏的组织——犹太教祭司体系与罗马国家。祂宣讲天国的道理；祂训练自己去信奉灵魂的绝对王权，祂自己灵魂的绝对王权；祂告诉我们，每个人都当学会在自己的思想里、自己的幻象里寻得平安。这是极难做到的事；最难的是相信：我至高的幸福，就在于我对一切美的事物的领会。倘若我独自一人看日出，看晚间的星星出来，或者感受到男人或女人的爱——我本该对自己说：「此外别无他物。」可是人们不肯止步于此；他们不知足，永远追逐着一个在面前飞逃的影子，一个永远兑现不了承诺的身外之物。

我说，基督教本质上是无名者与孤独者的宗教，是那些不成功者的宗教。它是这样一种人的宗教：一生思虑甚多，交游甚少，而其思想也一无所成。同一主题我还讲了许多，如今都忘了。


===Original===
After the service was over I went down into the vestry. Nobody came near me but my landlord, the chapel-keeper, who said it was raining, and immediately went away to put out the lights and shut up the building. I had no umbrella, and there was nothing to be done but to walk out in the wet. When I got home I found that my supper, consisting of bread and cheese with a pint of beer, was on the table, but apparently it had been thought unnecessary to light the fire again at that time of night. I was overwrought, and paced about for hours in hysterics. All that I had been preaching seemed the merest vanity when I was brought face to face with the fact itself; and I reproached myself bitterly that my own creed would not stand the stress of an hour’s actual trial.

Towards morning I got into bed, but not to sleep; and when the dull daylight of Monday came, all support had vanished, and I seemed to be sinking into a bottomless abyss. I became gradually worse week by week, and my melancholy took a fixed form. I got a notion into my head that my brain was failing, and this was my first acquaintance with that most awful malady hypochondria. I did not know then what I know now, although I only half believe it practically, that this fixity of form is a frequent symptom of the disease, and that the general weakness manifests itself in a determinate horror, which gradually fades with returning health.

For months﻿—many months﻿—this dreadful conviction of coming idiocy or insanity lay upon me like some poisonous reptile with its fangs driven into my very marrow, so that I could not shake it off. It went with me wherever I went, it got up with me in the morning, walked about with me all day, and lay down with me at night. I managed, somehow or other, to do my work, but I prayed incessantly for death; and to such a state was I reduced that I could not even make the commonest appointment for a day beforehand. The mere knowledge that something had to be done agitated me and prevented my doing it.

In June next year my holiday came, and I went away home to my father’s house. Father and mother were going, for the first time in their lives, to spend a few days by the seaside together, and I went with them to Ilfracombe. I had been there about a week, when on one memorable morning, on the top of one of those Devonshire hills, I became aware of a kind of flush in the brain and a momentary relief such as I had not known since that November night. I seemed, far away on the horizon, to see just a rim of olive light low down under the edge of the leaden cloud that hung over my head, a prophecy of the restoration of the sun, or at least a witness that somewhere it shone. It was not permanent, and perhaps the gloom was never more profound, nor the agony more intense, than it was for long after my Ilfracombe visit. But the light broadened, and gradually the darkness was mitigated. I have never been thoroughly restored. Often, with no warning, I am plunged in the Valley of the Shadow, and no outlet seems possible; but I contrive to traverse it, or to wait in calmness for access of strength.


===Chinese===
礼拜结束后，我走进楼下的法衣室。除房东、那位看门人外，无人走近我；他说了句「下雨了」，便即刻走去熄灯锁门。我没有带伞，只好冒雨走出去。到家时，我的晚饭——面包、干酪，外加一品脱啤酒——已摆在桌上，但显然，谁都觉得夜到那般时辰没有必要再生火了。我身心俱疲，歇斯底里地在屋里来回踱了几个钟头。一旦与事实本身面面相对，我讲道的全部内容似乎都成了十足的虚妄；我痛责自己，自己的信条竟经不起一个钟头实际试炼的重压。

挨到天快亮我才上床，却睡不着；星期一晦暗的日光到来时，一切支撑都已消散，我仿佛正沉入一口无底的深渊。我一周坏似一周，忧郁定下了一个固定的形状。我脑子里生出一个念头：我的脑子正在坏掉。这是我头一次结识疑病症（hypochondria）这最可怕的疾病。我如今知道——尽管实际上只信一半——当时并不知道：这种固定形状正是此病常见的症候；整体的虚弱会显形为某种明确的恐怖，随着健康的恢复而逐渐消退。

一连数月——许许多多个月——这种即将变成白痴或疯子的可怕确信伏在我身上，像一条毒蛇，毒牙直刺入我的骨髓，甩也甩不脱。我到哪里它跟到哪里，早晨随我起身，白天陪我走动，夜里伴我躺下。我总算勉强把职务撑了下来，却无时无刻不在祈求死亡；我被折磨到这般田地，连最寻常的事也不能提前一天约定。仅仅知道有一件事要做，便足以令我心慌意乱，做不下去。

次年六月，假期到了，我回父亲家中去。父母有生以来第一次要一同去海边住几天，我随他们去了伊尔弗勒科姆（Ilfracombe）。到那里约莫一星期，在一个难忘的早晨，在德文郡（Devonshire）一座山冈的顶上，我觉察到脑中一阵微微的发热、一阵片刻的宽舒——自那个十一月的夜晚以来，这样的感觉我还不曾有过。我仿佛在遥远的地平线上，就在笼罩我头顶的铅灰色云块的底缘之下，望见窄窄一线橄榄色的光，那是太阳将要归来的预言，或者至少是一个见证：它在什么地方依旧照耀。这光并不持久；伊尔弗勒科姆之行以后的很长一段时间里，或许阴翳从未更深，苦痛从未更烈。但光渐渐扩展，黑暗也随之缓缓消减。我从未彻底康复。我常常毫无预兆地跌入死荫的幽谷，不见任何出路；但我总能设法穿过去，或者安然等待力气的归来。


===Original===
When I was at my worst I went to see a doctor. He recommended me stimulants. I had always been rather abstemious, and he thought I was suffering from physical weakness. At first wine gave me relief, and such marked relief that whenever I felt my misery insupportable I turned to the bottle. At no time in my life was I ever the worse for liquor, but I soon found the craving for it was getting the better of me. I resolved never to touch it except at night, and kept my vow; but the consequence was, that I looked forward to the night, and waited for it with such eagerness that the day seemed to exist only for the sake of the evening, when I might hope at least for rest. For the wine as wine I cared nothing; anything that would have dulled my senses would have done just as well.

But now a new terror developed itself. I began to be afraid that I was becoming a slave to alcohol; that the passion for it would grow upon me, and that I should disgrace myself, and die the most contemptible of all deaths. To a certain extent my fears were just. The dose which was necessary to procure temporary forgetfulness of my trouble had to be increased, and might have increased dangerously.

But one day, feeling more than usual the tyranny of my master, I received strength to make a sudden resolution to cast him off utterly. Whatever be the consequence, I said, I will not be the victim of this shame. If I am to go down to the grave, it shall be as a man, and I will bear what I have to bear honestly and without resort to the base evasion of stupefaction. So that night I went to bed having drunk nothing but water. The struggle was not felt just then. It came later, when the first enthusiasm of a new purpose had faded away, and I had to fall back on mere force of will. I don’t think anybody but those who have gone through such a crisis can comprehend what it is. I never understood the maniacal craving which is begotten by ardent spirits, but I understood enough to be convinced that the man who has once rescued himself from the domination even of half a bottle, or three-parts of a bottle of claret daily, may assure himself that there is nothing more in life to be done which he need dread.

Two or three remarks begotten of experience in this matter deserve record. One is, that the most powerful inducement to abstinence, in my case, was the interference of wine with liberty, and above all things its interference with what I really loved best, and the transference of desire from what was most desirable to what was sensual and base. The morning, instead of being spent in quiet contemplation and quiet pleasures, was spent in degrading anticipations. What enabled me to conquer, was not so much heroism as a susceptibility to nobler joys, and the difficulty which a man must encounter who is not susceptible to them must be enormous and almost insuperable. Pity, profound pity, is his due, and especially if he happen to possess a nervous, emotional organisation. If we want to make men water-drinkers, we must first of all awaken in them a capacity for being tempted by delights which water-drinking intensifies. The mere preaching of self-denial will do little or no good.

Another observation is, that there is no danger in stopping at once, and suddenly, the habit of drinking. The prisons and asylums furnish ample evidence upon that point, but there will be many an hour of exhaustion in which this danger will be simulated and wine will appear the proper remedy. No man, or at least very few men, would ever feel any desire for it soon after sleep. This shows the power of repose, and I would advise anybody who may be in earnest in this matter to be specially on guard during moments of physical fatigue, and to try the effect of eating and rest. Do not persist in a blind, obstinate wrestle. Simply take food, drink water, go to bed, and so conquer not by brute strength, but by strategy.

Going back to hypochondria and its countless forms of agony, let it be borne in mind that the first thing to be aimed at is patience﻿—not to get excited with fears, not to dread the evil which most probably will never arrive, but to sit down quietly and *wait*. The simpler and less stimulating the diet, the more likely it is that the sufferer will be able to watch through the wakeful hours without delirium, and the less likely is it that the general health will be impaired. Upon this point of health too much stress cannot be laid. It is difficult for the victim to believe that his digestion has anything to do with a disease which seems so purely spiritual, but frequently the misery will break up and yield, if it do not altogether disappear, by a little attention to physiology and by a change of air. As time wears on, too, mere duration will be a relief; for it familiarises with what at first was strange and insupportable, it shows the groundlessness of fears, and it enables us to say with each new paroxysm, that we have surmounted one like it before, and probably a worse.


===Chinese===
病到最重的时候，我去看了医生。他劝我服用兴奋剂。我素来饮食有节，他便认为我害的是身体虚弱。起初，酒给了我宽舒，而且那样显著，以致每当觉得苦楚难忍，我便去寻酒瓶。我一生从不曾因酒误事，但很快便发现对酒的渴求正在占了上风。我决心只在夜里沾酒，并且守住了誓言；可结果是，我盼着黑夜，等它等得那样急切，白天仿佛只为夜晚而存在——到了夜里，我总算可以指望歇息了。酒本身如何，我毫不在意；任何能麻木我感官的东西，效果都一样。

可如今，一种新的恐怖生了出来。我开始害怕自己正在变成酒的奴隶；害怕这嗜欲会越长越盛，我会身败名裂，死得比一切死法都更可鄙。我的恐惧在某种程度上是有道理的：为换取一时的忘忧，酒量不得不一加再加，而且本可能加到危险的地步。

可是有一天，我格外感到这主子的暴虐，忽然得着力量，立下一个决绝的心愿，要将它彻底抛开。无论后果如何，我对自己说，我决不做这耻辱的牺牲品。倘若我注定要进坟墓，我也要作为一个人进去；我要诚实地承担我必须承担的，绝不乞灵于自我麻醉这种卑怯的逃避。于是那天夜里，我只喝了水，便上床睡了。挣扎当时并不觉得。它是后来才来的——当新志向最初的热忱消退，我只得单凭意志力支撑的时候。我想，唯有亲身经历过这场危机的人，才能明白那是什么。烈酒所生的那种疯狂渴欲，我从未体会过，但我领教的已足以让我确信：一个人只要曾把自己从哪怕每天半瓶、乃至四分之三瓶红葡萄酒的统治下解救出来，他便可以确信，人生再没有什么他需要畏惧的事情了。

在这个问题上，有几句由经验生出的体会值得记下。其一：就我而言，戒酒最有力的动因，是酒对自由的干涉，尤其是它干涉了我真正至爱的东西，把欲念从最可欲的事物转移到官能而卑下的东西上去。于是清晨不再用于安静的沉思与安静的乐趣，而是耗于自贬的期待之中。使我得胜的，与其说是英雄气概，不如说是对更高尚的快乐仍有感受力；一个对此没有感受力的人所要遭遇的困难，必然巨大而几乎不可克服。他应得的怜悯——深切的怜悯——尤其是倘若他恰巧生着一套敏感易激的神经构造。我们若想让人成为饮水者，首先必须在他们心中唤醒一种能力，能被饮清水所增进的那类快乐所引动。单单宣讲克己，收效甚微，甚至全无用处。

另一条观察是：一下子骤然戒掉饮酒的习惯，并没有危险。监狱和疯人院在此点上提供了充分的证据；但总会出现许多筋疲力尽的时刻，那种危险会被模拟出来，酒便显得是对症的良药。没有一个人——至少极少有人——会在睡醒后不久还想喝酒。这说明了休息的力量；我愿奉劝凡在此事上认真的人，在身体疲乏的时刻要格外警惕，不妨试试进食和休息的效果。不要一味盲目地死命硬搏。只管吃东西，喝水，上床睡觉——这样取胜靠的不是蛮力，而是谋略。

回到疑病症及其无数形态的苦痛上来：请记住，头一件要求得的是忍耐——不要因恐惧而激动，不要畏惧那多半永远不会降临的祸事，而要安静地坐下*等待*。饮食越简单、越少刺激性，病人就越有可能清醒地熬过无眠的时辰而不致谵妄，总体健康受损害的可能也越小。健康这一点，怎样强调都不过分。病人很难相信，他的消化竟会同一种看似纯粹属灵的疾病有关；但稍加注意生理、换一换空气，那苦痛往往便会松动、让步，即便不完全消失。而且随着时间推移，仅仅时间本身便是一种宽解；因为它使起初陌生而难堪的东西变得熟惯，它显出种种恐惧的毫无根据，它使我们每逢新的发作都能说：同样的我从前熬过去过一次，而且多半更凶的一次也熬过去过。
